test is to try to rest the instep of the foot on the pedal at its lowest. If this can't be done lower the saddle at once. A small frame with a high saddle pillar is not really as effective as a larger frame with less of the pillar protruding. True, the actual distance from the bracket to the saddle is exactly the same in both cases, but in the former the rather unnatural position throws an undue strain on the- frame -and makes comfortable, riding almost impossible. For growing bOys and girls it is pai- ticularly desirable to have the longest—but not too long—frame possible at the time to allow for future growth, A NOTE ON CHANGING GEAR.

Comparatively few motor cyclists appreciate the true importance of changing gear at the right moment. There is an exact moment when it should be done. In a very great number of cases it is delayed much too long. Perhaps this matter is not of such vital importance to riders of powerful- engined machines, but it is a point that deserves consideration from those who are mounted on low- powered motor cycles. I have recently been mak- ing notes on this subject, from observing riders in a hilly district in which_ I have been staying, and I have come to the conclusion that the change should be brought about at least fifty yards before the majority of cyclists think it necessary. The best way of determining the psychological moment is to ride in Company with an expert who is mounted on a similar make of machine. This will demonstrate the advisability of changing earlier. If a speedometer is fitted on the cycle this may be used for guidance.. When the speed drops to a given point, change thettear; if the needle remains' steady the right point was selected. If the needle swings upwards the change was made too late. ADJUSTING TAPPETS.

The satisfactory running of a-car depends in a very large measure upon the careful and correct adjustment of .the 'tappets. -Although many motor- ists regard it as. an extremely .difficult matter, .it is not 16 ha reality, and there is no mason at all Why. .any capable motorist! should not attempt the; work for himself. 'Yet I know Many who, regardit,,as. an exceedingly difficult matter, and never Under-, ta.ke the work *themselves.' The smallest passible clearance between the valve stem and the top .of the tappet is the ,ideal. There ahOuld only be a very slight •Movement -of the taPpet.-., The, only tools required are a couple of spanners, while the time 'occupied ,by, the adjustment is reckoned in. seconds and not in minutes. One, spanner holds the nut" on the head of the tappet and the other the lock nut. The latter is slacked .a turn, and the tappet is then screwed out until the required clearance has been secured. The lock nut must then be tightened so as to prevent the tappet from moving, for if this occurs the amount of clearance would be increased.

SPOKES.

A FREE BOOK FOR CYCLISTS.

The 1916 edition of "The Book of the Raleigh" is now published, and follows the lines which have made it foi years a handbook to the high-grade bicycle. The pages devoted to the constructional features of the all-steel bicycle are full of interest to the rider, and show the high pitch which the maliufacture of the bicycle has now reached in this country. Parts which, in other bicycles, are made of malleable iron castings, are in the Raleigh made of the finest homogeneous cold-rolled steel, pressed into form by giant power presses, capable of exerting a pressure of a thousand tons to the square inch. The immense, strength thus secured is but one of the many special features of the popular all-steel Raleigh, whose fame is now literally known "from China. to Peru." For in- stance, in addition to being supplied to His Majesty's Government and many British and Colonial regiments, the Raleigh is now sold in places so widely apart as Armenia and Japan, Nigeria and Siam, Mexico and the Malay States— indeed, wherever the bicycle is known. "The Book of the Raleigh" can be obtained free from any of the Company's 29 depots or 2,000 agents, or it will be sent post free to any reader who will forward his or her address on a postcard to the Rileigh Cycle Co., Ltd., Nottingham, and as it is full of interest for everyone interested in cycling, we hope that our readers will take full advantage of this opportunity of securing a cyclist's rade mecum and send for a copy.

CYCLING AND MOTORING.

GOOD BRAKES ESSENTIAL.

A good brake is perhaps the most important part et a bicycle; certainty there is 'no part of greater importance. It is merely courting a bad spill sooner or later—generally sooner—to ride a machine with a defective brake. Brake failures have always been the most prolific cause of accidents. And they are likely to remain so until cyclists come to realise that a few minutes' trouble is infinitely better than risking an accident. In a good machine the brake is usually one of the strongest parts of the machine. but no amount of mechanical perfection will coun- teract the effect of carelessness and continued neg- lect. After a hard 'season's riding brake rubbers often refuse to grip the rims, as the result of wear, but they only require a minor adjustment, or at most renewal, to ensure safety. Again, screws may have worked loose or rods 'become defective; yet many riders never think such details worth at- tending to. In any case no one should ever trust to a single brake; two are always necessary, and either should be capable of bringing the machine to a standstill under the most exacting conditions. In emergency, the foot can be used as a very effec- tive brake, though to the detriment of the tyre, and the trick of using it should always be learnt in case it is ever needed. THE HEIGHT TO RIDE.

One of the most common faults made by begin- ners—and even experienced riders are not always immune from blame—is that of riding with too high a saddle. Especially is this the case with children who come into possession of a machine formerly belonging to one of their elders. Time after time I have seen boys and girls vainly trying to reach the pedals of a machine much too big for them, and have wondered who was responsible. The Recruit and His Luck.

By a piece of good luck the new recruit had been appointed orderly to his captain, and the latter was now giving him his instructions. "You rise at five o'clock," he said, "shave yourself, and clean your boots and equipment. Then clean my boots, buttons, belt, &c., shave me, see to my • horse—which you must groom thoroughly—and clean the equipment. After that you go to your hut, help to serve the break- fast, and after breakfast lend a hand washing- up. At eight o'clock you go on parade and drill till twelve—" . The recruit, whose face had been growing longer, then'said : " Beg pardon, sir, but is there anyone else in the Army besides me?"

THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, JUNE 10, 1916.

JOTTINGS BY AN OLD WHY DUKES AND SEABURY WERE JOURNALIST. REJECTED.

Rather Significant.

The item respecting walking sticks for the wounded somehow reminded me of one of the very few colleagues with whom I have been unable to work agreeably. He was not a favourite with other members of the staff, and when at last he confided to us that he was about to leave for another situation, there was general rejoicing. Each promptly and cheer- fully planked down his mite toward a parting gift, and when it was suggested that the testi- monial should take the form of an ebony walk- ing stick, it was unanimously agreed that this would be most appropriate in the circum- stances. "Walking Day" •in that instance afforded us all unfeigned pleasure. "Can Women Paint?"

This, which served as headline to an account of certain proceedings before the Lymm Tribunal, reminded me of an incident in the bygones. I had been sent to interview the lead- ing member of a theatrical company who were playing for a week in the town in which I was then stationed. I entered by the stage door, and was told that I should find my man in the wings. I started up the narrow staircase leading to the stage, and encountered-therein zne.of the ladies of the cast who, a turn evidentry ended, was hurriedly descending to her dressing room. It was a startling vision for a young reporter. The actress's make-up had required an extraordinary use of grease paints, and, seen thus at close quarters, she struck one as positively hideous. Can women paint?" Well, I should smile. Blind Ministers.

The appearance of a blind preacher in the pulpit of Lymm Congregational Church last Sunday set me thinking of many things. I remember reporting several discourses by a blind American Bishop who visited this country some forty years ago, and whose eloquence at- tracted multitudes. I was particularly im- pressed by his recitals of the Scripture lessons, whieh in-variably included one of the "great chapters in Isaiah; a passage like "Then shall the eyes of the blind be opened 11 was well I calculated to thrill sympathetic hearers.

Too Many Blind. That modern marvel, Helen Keller—with whose triumph over tremendous odds in the way of physical disability I must assume ,readers to have some knowledge—insists that in the cases of one-third of the sufferers, blindness might with due care have been prevented. When one considers how heavily-handicapped throughAifer are these sorely-afflicted fellow creatures, it is a shocking indictment, but assuredly it is well- founded. The greatest risk to the eyes appears to be at birth, and a doctor tells me he always gives those important organs immediate and special attention. The necessity of so doing has been abundantly confirmed during my Eye Hos- pital experiences; I have repeatedly heard doctors and head nurses, counselling anxious mothers, use such fateful words as, "Now, be sure to apply this carefully and constantly, and observe the directions strictly; otherwise the child will be blind."

Concerning Dancing. At Statham Festival the Grappenhall Brass Band will play for public dancing, and before that for garland dancing, &c. One is glad to note that fancy dancing is not likely to be allowed to be, left exclusively to professional exponents, though there is undoubtedly a tendency that way. The old-fashioned country- side revelry of rural Lancashire and Cheshire was very different in a spectacular sense from a modern village dance. No doubt, as suggested by one writer on the subject several years ago, it gave scope at times for a good deal of boister- ous fun of a rather rowdy kind; but on the whole, there was far more, skill required- in the dancers, and perhaps more genuine amusement for all parties concerned. Comparatively seldom now is the old Lancashire step-dance seen in its perfection. Sometimes (as the authority just quoted further observed) on the sanded floor of village inn, some " old stager " may be in- duced to stand‘ up and give a performance of the step-dance or clog-dance of his youth. And a very elaborate affair it is too, with heel.an.l- - glides„ skips, hops, and double shtifflis, wonderful to witness, and exceeding difficult to perform. Our friend lamented the practical dis- appearance of the old jigs and hornpipes (now "well-nigh become matters for history "), with other dances, such as frisks, joaks, and the like, whose names alone survive. In a few villages, such, for example, as far-famed " Cockey Moor," the old women are known on special occasions to favour with a species of jig, which should be seen to be- adequately comprehended; but it remains true that many wonderful old dances have long ago passed into the limbo of forgotten arts and been succeeded by less picturesque , creations—or, at least some old fogeys like the contributor of this weekly column think so.

Why Not? The late Mr. Willett, who pressed his day- light saving idea through good and evil report, and saw his chief champion in Parliament again and again laughed at and ridiculed and his measure contemptuously and unceremoniously sent to the rlghtabout, was triumphantly justified to all eternity when the once thought- less and inconsistent scoffers turned completely round and eagerly welcomed it to a man when it was urged that the reform was essential in the interests of war-time economy—as if the so easily avoidable waste was previously of no mo- ment! It seems not improbable that under the like pressure those who have been wont to con- demn the vegetarians "lock, stock, and barrel," may be induced to regard the cult somewhat' more Complacently. When at last week's meet- ing the Guardians were discussing the beef con- tractors' application for an extra 3d. per lb. on their supplies to the Workhouse, the Rev. A. M.' Mitchell asked why they should not do without meat altogether as he had done for years. Well, it is not unreasonable in the cir- cumstances, to appeal for the more frequent substitution of substantial and wholesome non- flesh dinners at such institutions. That were simply to ask that paupers might be put on a dietary level with many householders who have poor rates to pay. But there! To whisper ever so faintly in some ears even that certain public institutions should have margarine instead of butter is something like putting forth an nn- holy hand to touch the sacred ark. Yet multi- tudes of respectable outsiders, while not netles- Barfly sharing old Betty Higden's horror of the Workhouse or that manifested by the poor fellow who committed suicide recently, use only margarine nowadays. Some of us have tasted no butter and little flesh meat for a year or more; and, such as it is, our self-sacrifice is made systematically and with a well-defined and not unworthy object. Rate-supported and voluntary institutions should as far as possible without anything like serious deprivation help in this way to tide over the national crisis.

Vegetarianism. As to opinions in relation to the general prin- ciple for which the excellent Vicar of Burton- wood is ever the doughty champion, all, of course, may continue to enjoy the utmost free- dom. While as always gratefully accepting at the hands of the vegetarians such of their uP-to- date recipes as are obviously of service to the community as a whole, we may continue to poke fun at their pure and unadulterated faddism-- as 1 well remember was done at a pre-war conference on " Piet " in connection with the National Food Public Association. As I read at the time, one bright contribution to that most scintillating conference came from Dr. Shelley, who gave quite a new version of Mary's vcry- muph-paradied lamb. Here is a summary of the amusing story : A medical friend of his was most interested in the question of the relation of morals to diet. He had some doubt as to the truth of the generally accepted argument that much :neat eating was bad for the moral fibre of mankind and the beast of the field. So he ex- perimented with the appetite and the passions of a pet lamb. This lamb (Jane was her name) he fed -entirely on mutton chops! Jane was his constant companion; on their walks abroad the good doctor watched her closely out of the corner of his eye, expecting that at any moment she might turn and rend him limb from limb. In ,course Of time this cannibal lamb became a sheep; yet, except for an unwonted sparkle in her eye and manifest uneasiness whenever a bed of mint was approached. she showed no signs of evil doing or evil thinking. And she de- veloped finally into as placid an old lady sheep as ever graced a meadow, loving and kind and full of charitableness! What will the Rev. A. M. Mitchell think of this heresy?

Councillor Roberts Defines The Attitude Of The Town Council.

TRADES COUNCIL'S RETORT.

The Mayor reported that he had received notice that Mr. R. Rule had enlisted in Forces, and was therefore unable to act as a member of the Naval and Military War Pen- sions, &c., Committee, and that in consequence the Trade Unions and the Trades and Labour, Council had been invited to recommend a mem- ber in his stead. The -Mayor also reported that at a meeting of representatives of the Trade Unions and the Trades and Labour. Council, held on the 23rd instant, Mr. Charles Dukes and Mr. A. E. Sea. bury were again recommended for appointment the Naval and Military War Pensips, &c., Committee. Resolved, that such recommendation be nnt entertained. In the above terms the determination of the Town Council in General Purposes Committee not to accept the nomination of Messrs. Dukes and Seabury for seats on the Pensions Com- mittee was submitted for the approval in open Council at the monthly meeting on Tuesday evening. The adoption of the minutes was moved by the Mayor, seconded by Dr. Joseph.

COUNCILLOR ROBERTS'S EXPLANATION.

Councillor Henry Roberts, in an impassioned speech, which visibly carried the whole Council with it, said it had -been considered 'desirable that he should explain on behalf of the Council the reasons whick had actuated the Council .on two occasions in a practically unanimous man- ner to refuse to approve of the appointment of the two persons named. -

and only as late as last Easter there was a meet- ing of the British Socialist Party—l think they call themselves that, although there is very little British about it—in Manchester. This meeting was presided over by one of these in- dividuals. A man who has given more service to true Socialism for the last 30 years than any of these men who are seeking notoriety to-day could give if they lived to be 100 years, a man who has made sacrifices for what he believes to be the true principles of Socialism—Hynd- man—and some 20 to 30 others seceded from the meeting as a ,protest against the pacifist and anti-national siiirit displayed at this meeting, held under the presidency, of one of the two candidates for seats on our Naval and Military War Pensions Committee. And then what hap- pened? The British Socialist Party, presided over by Mr. Seabury, whom I should not know if I ;met him, sat for the best part of two days with closed doors. Why was secrecy necessary in a case like this? If they were anxious for the well-being of their fellow-men and the welfare of their country, surely they would have allowed the world to know what attitude they were taking up; but, like the craven cowards they are, they sat inside, with closed doors; for fear something should become known which might land some of them in gaol. (Laughter.) That is the true explanation. -

" Brazen-faced."

Soon after the war broke out, when Parlia- ment asked us, and the whole national spirit gas desirous, that every penny that could be saved should be saved; these two gentlemen, accom- panied by a third, forced a municipal contest in Warrington, costing the town between £6O and £7O. Of course, £6O or £7O of public money does not matter to them. • Their proportion of the subscription towards_ that sum would be repre- sented by a minus quantity, so far as one of them, at any rate, is concerned. No desire was evinced to assist their country in a financial direction; but, on the contrary—whether it was to gratify their vanity .or not I. don't know— 'they forced an election and got what they 6:- served—a most sound and severe thrashing. (Hear, hear.) I have no objection to that part of it—(laughter)—except the expenditure of the money. But on the night of the poll, in front of this old, historic building and in the presence of a large number of people, when the custom- ary loyal ceremonial of the singing of the National Anthem took place, both these men, brazen-faced as they were, stood there, in spite of , the protests of those who were alongside them and of others, and showed their abomin- able insolence to the King and to whet the King represents by remuining with their hats on—an act in itself contemptibly mean , and paltry, but certainly not the act of loyal spb- fects.

===== 0002601_19160610_art0015.txt | included=true | included =====
Council's Right to Veto.

In the first place, said Councillor Roberts, there seems to be an impression in some quar-. ters that because a certain meeting was held at which certain names were submitted to this Council in a certain order that this Council was, bound, as a matter of tourse, to accept those Dailies. That is a delusion. This Council has a perfect right—in 'fact it is invested by the Statutory Committee with the power—to make a selection from such persons as are recommended, and it is not bound in any shape or degree by the decision of the body which nominates in the first place the persons in question. The Coun- cil has acted absolutely within its rights in ro- fusing, on two occasions, to accept the nomina- tion of these two individuals. One reason why we object to their appoint- ment is the fact that at the two meetings to which I have referred, which were held for the purpose of recommending names to submit to this Council, it is notorious that the decision of those meetings did not carry out the inten- tion of Parliament, inasmuch as it did. not represent the true voice of Labour in this town on this question. (Hear, hear.) I make that statement advisedly, and I say that the Trades and Labour Council- does not comprise more than half of the trade unions of the town, big and little; and, further, that only an insignifi- cant proportion of the trade unions belong to the Trades and Labour Council. Practically the whole-. of the largest and most important members of the trade unions of the town are outside the Trades and Labour Council.

A Travesty of Democracy.

Again, the Trades and Labour Council profess to be a democratic body. There never was a greater travesty of democratic principles than what was *done at those twol meetings. The Trades and Labour Council sent to the meetings its own representatives and took care to secure representatives from each of the unions affiliated to itself so that the Trades and Labour CoUncil, so far as its own opinions and delsires are con- cerned, practically got double representation at those meetings; hence the farcical results which followed. Does anyone who knows the opinion of Warrington imagine that if a popular vote was taken from trade unionist and labour men that they would have returned either of the two men mentioned? I venture to say that they would have been hopelessly beaten by a vote of the town. (Hear, hear.) Then another thing which prevented a real, adequate and proper view of the trade unionist opinion being taken is the fact that the extremely short time avail- able between the receipt of the notice convening the meeting and the holding of it, made it quite .i,mpossible for the various trade unions to meet to appoint their representatives and furnish them with instructions how• to vote. As a matter of fact practically only two, or at the I most four, unions were able to act constitu- tionally in their appointment of delegates to those meetings. So much for the Trades and ILabour Council.

===== 0002601_19160610_art0016.txt | included=true | included =====
The. Question of Loyalty.

But our second and -great objection to the appointment of these two men is that they are not believed to be loyal subjects of the King and country. (Hear, hear.) Why do I say that? I say that out of the evidence of their own mouths. I have no interest personally as applied to either of these two men in raising this question, but every member of this Council is sworn in his allegiance to the King to do all that he believes right, proper and just for the preservation of his Majesty's position and the integrity of the country. (Hear, hear.) r say without any fear of contradiction that this is no time for parleying with the great circum- stances of the hour. The facts of the last few days, and particularly of to-day, make it more imperative than ever that every man who loves his country and every man who respects his King shall do all that lies in his power to make it impossible for men who owe no allegiance to King or country to hold any position of public importance in any direction whatever. We will have no part or lot in the elevation of such men at this time. They don't love England, and they care less for England and England's soldiers and sailors. Then let them get out of England and -find some prace more congenial to them. (Hear, hear.) This country belongs to those who love England, and who are loyal to its interests and are willing to fight for its in- terests, and we are going to see to it, when the time is more ripe than it is at the present moment, that a proper sense of proportion is obtained in regard to the different positions which men of different views shall occupy. (Hear, hear.)

===== 0002601_19160610_art0017.txt | included=true | included =====
Conscience.

We oppose the appointment of the two in- dividuals in question on the ground that they are not loyal. In proof of that I have only to refer you to the local Press. They and their association opposed recruiting, and they declined to have anything to do with Lord Derby's scheme, or to give the slightest assistance in any direction whatever to bring together the men necessary for preserving this country of ours from invasion and from dangers of a s‘imi- lar nature. And then we have the pettifogging and paltry excuse made by one of them that he would not have it on his conscience—his conscience! what is his conscience worth ?—that he had advised any young man to take up arms for fear he might lose his life. Are not our men losing their lives by thousands? (Hear, hoar.) What aboht those, magnificent fellows who went down last week without a murmur, in order, that England's hearths and homes Might be saved from the dangers of invasion? What about the man whose life we know so much about, whose memory we venerate, who to-day has made the great sacrifice of all for England? (Hear, hear.) And then for a man who, up to now, has used every dodge and trick he knows of to evade his proper responsibility as a citizen of this country, to excuse himself on the ground that his conscience—God help his conscience!_. won't permit him to do this thing for fear be should have the bloOd of som4 young man upon his shoulders! We will take it for what it is worth.

Socialists in Camera..

And then they had the audacitY, and I use the word advisedly, after refusing to assist in pro- tecting t ngland-against its enemies, to demand seats on a Tribunal which should adjudge who should be excused, and who would not be. You, Mr. Mayor, very properly said that those who had made Tribunals necessary had no right t" ask- for seats upon them. (Hear, hear-) Then they opposed the Military Service Bill;

===== 0002601_19160610_art0019.txt | included=true | included =====
"A Damnable Slander."

ButoSir, that is not all. In 1914 there was a paper published in Warrington called the "Labour Gazette." The editor's name is given as C. Dukes, and I believe I am right in saying —I am -subject to ,correction—that the other illtistrious colleague-of Mr. Dukes was assisting; to some extent, in the production of this paper. At any rate, in the very same num- ber '' of this paper is an article Which bears his initials. Ile was, there- fore, a contributor, and to that extent, I take it, his views were in accord with the views of the editor. In that number thie:-pamphlet, copied and placed in the editorial columns, appears. It is, headed ." The Military Ideal," audit is addressed to the young men of England. This is haw it reads:—

Young man The lowest aim in your life is te be a good- soldier; Ther"good, soldier" n6cer tries to distinguish right from wrong. He never thinks, never reasons; he only obeys. If he is Ordered to fire on his fellow-citizens, n his friends, on his neighbours, he obeys with- out hesitation. If he is ordered to fire down a crowded street when the poor are clamour- ing for bread he obeys and sees the grey hairs of age stained with red and the life tide gush- ing from the breast of woman, feels neither remorse nor sympathy. If he is ordered off as one of a firing squad to execute a hero or benefactor, he fires without hesitation, though he knows the bullet will pierce the noblest heart that ever beat in human breast. A good soldier is a blind, heartless, soulless, murderous machine. He is not a man, he is not even a brute, for brutes only kill in gel- defence. All that is human in him, all that is divine in him, all that constitutes a man, has been sworn away when he took the enlist- ment oath. His mind, his conscience, and his very soul are in the keeping of his officer. No man can fall lower than a soldier—it is a depth beneath which we cannot go. Young man: Don't be a soldier or a Porn- tonal; be a man.

Cries of "Shame," which were joined in by the public present in the Council Chamber. "That Is Not The Worst Of It." You may Well say "Shame," continued Coun- cillor Roberts. A more disgraceful, a more damnable slander on the bravest men who ever left England's shores never was uttered, or printed. (Hear, hear.) And this is the kind of thing, that emanates from the official organ of the Trades and Labour Council—Charles Dukes, editor. But that is not the worst of it. In "The Times" of March 20th, 1915, this state- ment appears:—

The British working man is probably un- aware of the extent to which German influence has been employed to foment industrial un- rest. An instance of this is to be found in a pernicious leaflet, a copy of which has come into our handt, as having been circulated withiff the last few days both in London.. and. Glasgow.. We have made inquiries about it. . _

and find that the authorities have known of it existence since January, 1914, when 25,000 copies of it were printed for circulation by members of "The Industrial Worlgers'

League," of 166, Farringdon-street, E.C. It bears no imprint, but the printer has been discovered in one Valentine Frietag, a Ger- man, who is also the printer of "The Herald of Revolt," "The Industrial Worker," "The Catering World," and other revolution- ary literature. He is now in the hands of the police. I believe it is right to say that this circular was issued broadcast at the time of the dock strike in London, and at the time of the strike amongst munition workers in Glasgow. I verily believe that the printing and circula- tion of that leaflet was a part pf the German propaganda, paid for by German money. I don't say it was consciously introduced into this "Labour Gazette" with knowledge of that fact, hut the fact that it is printed without an im- print and by a German who is now incarcerated, goes a long way to justify my surmise that German money had a great deal to do with the introduction of that pamphlet in the first in- stance,

"Enslavement of the Working Classes." A short time ago a No-Conscription Fellow- ship card was obtained from Mr. Seabury's office—a card which pledges the subscriber, to its contents—not to take up arms in case of conscription whatever the, penalties are for re- fusing. From the same source a copy of a reser lution evidently prepared for submission to the trade union concerned was obtained.

This resolution ran:— That this meeting of the Warrington Branch of the National Union of Gas and General Labourers do hereby protest against the tendency, of the Government towards enforcing a system of military and industrial conscrip- tion—[There is no, and has never been the slightest tendency on the part of the Govern- ment towards industrial conscriptionl—and regards such a''tendency to a further enslave- ment of the working classes, a shackling of , the few liberties we at present Possess—[What I wonder, would be the enslavement of the working classes if they were left to the tender mercies of the type of men I am discussing?)_ and a subjection of all that lends to social evolution and do hereby pledge ourselves to resist by any and every means any attempts

===== 0002601_19160610_art0020.txt | included=true | included =====
at diversion from the system at present in operation." The 11"ght of the Maimed.

I contend that the Council has ample justi- fication—in fact, I say it is laid upon the Council as an imperative duty that they shall not in the slightest degree pander to the spirit which has actuated these men in taking up the atti- tude they have adopted. Remembering what we do of the purposes and objects for Nvtich this Pensions Committee is about to be formed, remembering as we do that there wril have to come before it in Warrington possibly scores of men—fellow-workers of ours, fellow-townsmen and citizens of ours, some of thenr old school fellows, and some of them rela- tives of ours—don't you think, don't we' all think that they are at least entitled to come before a committee composed absolutely of men and women who not only sympathise with them because of the maimed condition which has be- fallen them in the performance of their duty to their King and country; but who also appreciate all the sacrifices they have made to save England from the fate which has befallen Belgium. (Applause.) The Mayor asked for further comments,. but thought a sufficiently strong case had been made out to justify a solid vote. The minutes were carried unanimously.

===== 0002601_19160610_art0021.txt | included=true | included =====
THE OTHER SIDE.

Mr. Dukes Looks Into Coun- cillor Roberts's Record.

Within an hour of Councillor Henry Roberts having delivered his speech, Mr. Charles Dukes and Mr. A. E. Seabury were making their reply' before the Trades and Labour Council, congre- gated in the Socialist Hall, Bridge Foot. Mr. Dukes had been side-tracked from attend- ing the Town Council meeting owing to a business engagement, but had received from Mr. Sea. bury the gist of the attack, that gentleman-hav- ing been in the strangers' gallery during the debate.

Mr. Dukes. said he had no desire to be per- sonal, but he understood that those who had taken objection to him and Mr. Seabury had done so mainly in the name of Labour, had attached great importance to the past record of the men to whom they objected, and has' per- suaded the Town Council to, vote against the acceptance of the nominees of the joint meet- ing of trade union officials. He (Mr. Dukes) had no objection to anyone looking into his past record as a Labour man, for he had never pre- tended to be anything else, but if hi i; record was to be looked into and to be judged as to his fitness'to sit upon the War Pensions Committee, 'then he was-fully entitled to examine the his- tory' of those who had taken objection to his co-option. He had ever taken the view that the main function of a Labour man was the con- sideration of Labour questions, and to confine himself to the study of the interests of the class he claimed to represent. He could not be accused of veering from that position. It was, therefore, desirable that they should know from whom the objection arose that Mr. Seabury and he were not fitted as Labour men to sit upon that committee. He had no objection to the views held by Mr. Roberts, nor to those of any- body else, providing they did not claim to dic- tate what the views and opinions of Labour men should be. In January of. 1914 a confer- ence of the Labour party declared- Labour's views on the war, passing a resolution urging the workers to resist the efforts of the great war trusts to increase expenditure on armaments and calling upon parents to withstand the "insidious attempts of' the 'National Service League to introduce a system of conscription " ; the party also advocating between the workers of the world an alliance against war. Militarist in Every Sense. .

"Now the man who calls in question my views as a Labour man was one of the chief propa- gandists of the National Service League," de- clared Mr. Dukes. "Mr. Roberts favoured com- pulsoyy military 'training prior to the war and openly advocated it. He was a militarist in every sense of the word, and those who look up the booklets of that League will see that the origin of the schemes of military training they propound are to be found in the country we are fighting to-day. I have no objection to a Labdur man criticising my views, but, I certainly object to a man who has been a militarist to his bask bone. lam one of those who have stuck to the Labour party's resolution of 1914, and I object to men of Mr. Roberts's calibre, who, if they had had their way, would have Germanised England years and years ago." (Applause.) Protesting that the Trades Council was repre- sentative of Labour, and that the joint meet- ing which nominated Mr. Seabury and himself was also representative, Mr. Dukes continued. I want you to note that it is a representative of the employing class who objects to the men who should represent Labour on the committee. I openly admit that there is not, and there can- not be, any social affinity between men of Labour who understand Labour, and those who exploit Labour. I have never pretended to represent that indefinable quantity, public opinion, any more than I have represented the views of the property-owning class, and if you assume that the benefit of the workers lies in taking the hand of these people you are deplor- ably mistaken, as the future will prove. The November Elections.

"Reference has been made to the November elections. I want you to remember by whom a truce was declared. It was one thing to declare a truce, say, in Manchester, where, whilst you are not adequately represented, you have at least a fair number of Labour men on the Council; it was another thing to declare a truce which means Liberal and Tor/ domination. (Hear, hear.) That matter, I contend, is totally apart from the subject at issue." Their wish to be on the War Pensions Com- mittee had been described as audacious, added the speaker, who asked them to compare that with the audacity of an employer of Labour who sought to dictate to Labour who its,repre- sentatives should be. "I ask Mr. Roberts to name to me publicly anything in Prussianism which can out-Prussianise ' that. How would he like us to dictate as to who should sit with him on a Board of Directors?" (Applause.)

Reference had been made as to what occurred at the British Socialist party conference. Had that anything to do with the matter? He won- deredif H. M. Hyndman, who had been named, would' care to rub shoulders with Mr. Roberts? .A. ,Member: He would not: .

• Mr. Dukes: If I know Hyndman, he would not. What had been said about the B.S.P. con- ference showed that there was the most abject ignorance regarding what took place there. Their "Criminality."

Mr. Dukes declared that there was not one institution or society, excepting Labour, over which Mr. Roberts could not wield an influence in the appointments for the War Pensions Com- mittee. When the whole of the criminality of Mr. Seabury and himself had been summed up it meant that they had conceded to every dividual the same rights and privileges they wished for themselves—the right to elercise his own faculty as to where his duty lay. They had no more right to coerce a man in a matter of that kind than they had, to beat him physically. All he (Mr. Dukes) regretted was that the per- sonalities introduced should have occupied so much time, public thought and opinion and if the men who claimed to be so anxious lo serve their country were as genuine as their utter- ances would imply, then, he declared, there was more useful, better and urgent work for them to do than by constantly handling the "muck rake." The reason their opponents attacked them was because they believed that this time of crisis gave them a favorable opportunity to undermine' the little vestige of Labour organisa- tion that yet remained. But, he warned them that no one knew what the future had in store. If their love of country were to mean any- thing useful in any degree, it should be exer- Aged not in fomenting trouble and the creating of bad blood, but in the consideration of the great problems which would have to be faced on the morrow. (Applause.)

MR. SEABURY CONTRADICTORY.

Mr. Seabury said that -Mr. Roberts had made statements which were untruthful. The first one was that the Trades and Labour Council did not represent one-half of the trade union move- ment in Warrington. As a matter of fact, the number of societies in Warrington was 56, and the 33 affiliated had the largest numbers in the town. Another statement was that the joint meetings at which the Labour men had been nominated was unrepresentative of Labour, and the fact given for this was that the time be- tween the notifications of the meetings being sent to the societies and he meetings being called was insufficient for the 'members of the

unions to be consulted. But that was entirer3r due to others and not the societies summoo.ed. It was true that a number of the officials did not bring the matter before their various, branches, but there were several which did so, and came back with a definite mandate for the resolution endorsed at the Town Hall meeting. It had also been,stated that in the Warrington office of the Gas Workers and General Labourers' Union an individual had been sup- plied by himself with the membership of the No-Conscription Fellowship. That was abso- lutely untrue, for he had never held an official position in the Fellowship, although he would say that he did not regret his association with it.

It had also been stated that a resolution had been passed at a meeting of the branch of the Gas Workers' Union declaring their opposition to military and industrial conscription, and pledging the meeting to resist by any and every means any attempt at deviation from the system *then in operation. Unless the resolution had been stolen from the office of that union it could not have got into the hands of Mr. Roberts. But as far as the resolution was con- ' cerned, it was passed on January Ist, and had been endorsed by every recognised Labour body in the country, prior, of course, to the introduc- tion of the Compulsory Service Act. A Mock Election.

Mr. Shimmins (Postmen's Federation) said that as a represettative of 'a branch which op-

posed Mr. Dukes tooth and nail over his atti- tude to the Compulsory Service Act, he could now say that the postmen were unanimous that Mr. Dukes should be on the War Pensions Com- mittee. If it came to a vote between Dukes and Roberts by the Labour men of Warrington, he had not the slightest doubt as to who would come out on top. (Applause.)

It was announced that the Executive Com- mittee of the Trades Council had forwarded the whole facts to the Central Statutory Committee, and these had been formally, acknowledged. A protest was 'made against the nominations which the National Workers' League had sent to the Town Council, it being contended that the League was political, and if they had the right to nominate anyone the Independent Labour party, the British Socialist party, or any other political organisation should have the same privilege.

SUPPLEMENTARY HELP. Not To Be The Subject Of Charitable Appeals.

At the Town Council on Tuesday the Mayor moved :

(1) That in the opinion of this Council (a) any moneys required to supplement the pensions, grants, and allowances to the widows and other dependants of our sailors and soldiers, or to disabled men, should be provided by the State, and should not be the subject of charitable appeals; and (b) the expenses of the Local and District Committees constituted under the provisions of the Naval and Military War Pensions, &e., Act, 1915, should be borne by the State, and should not be a charge on the rates.

(2) That a copy of the foregoing resolutiOn be forwarded to the Chancellor of the Exche- quer, the President* of the Local Government Board, the. Member• for the Borough, and .the Secretary Of the Statutory Committee. The Mayor explained that the resolution had been passed by the Town Council of Chester and other Corporations had been asked to support it. Among the functions of the local PensionS Committee, continued his Worship, the power or duty is imposed "to solicit and receive from the public contributions towards the purposes of the Act." I take it that any Corporation can appeal for local contributions without anY special cower being given to them by the Act. The main function prescribed is, " to distribute any supplementary grants made by the Statu- tory Committee, the distribution of which has been delegated to the local committee." The Statutory Committee has already received 21,000,000 out of public funds, and we- are quite prepared to distribute our share of sueh money among the people entitled to it in this town. If the Statutory Committee require further funds they should come from the same source, i.e., out of the National Exchequer. (Hear, hear.) Local Funds Should Be Guarded.

It is all very well to appeal for local-sub- scriptions, but we all know that such an ap- peal would enable many people who could well afford and ought to contribute to evade their duty and responsibility. The local committee is also asked to help the Statutory Committee with any funds they may have at their dis- posal. We have, it is true, a load relief fund at present amounting to £1,680, but I doubt whether the Corporation would be willing to use this money in supplementing the Govern- ment allowances, and for the present I am strongly opposed to the idea. (Hear, hear.) As showing the disposition of the Statutory Com- mittee to avail themselves of all the local funds they can secure, they issued on the 60. May last a strong appeal to all Pensions Committees to raise voluntary funds, and this they have asked the local committees to do without first proposing to distribute the funds which they have in hand. To my mind this is a singular course of procedure and a course which local committees will have seriously to watch and guard against if they wish to avoid the duty and the direct cost of providing for those whom the Act professes to help and provide for. (Hear, hear.) , Expenses A National Charge. -

Then, with regard to the second resolution, the expenses of administration by the local committees are to be a charge upon the funds "at the disposal of the local committee, except so far as they may be paid by the Statutory Committee." This apparently gives to the Statutory Committee the option of paying or refusing to contribute to the administration expenses of the local committees. The Pensions Committee will not only have to provide offices. but they will require the services of a clerk, inquiry officers and probably, other officials. The committee cannot rely solely upqn volun- tary help in the discharge of these duties, and in my opinion it is not right that the expenses which will have to be incurred should be made a charge upon the local rates. (Hear, hear.) Dr. Joseph seconded the resolution, which was unanimously adopted.

Some Difference. "When I went to Washington," said the congressman who was defeated, ." the people of Plunkville gathered and strewed roses in no path." "And when you returned as a lame duck?" "They strewed banana peelings and cactus!"

TO BE DIVIDED.

Bishop Of Liverpool On Big Warrington Parishes.

Speaking to a conference of clergy and laity t the Deanery of Winwick, in the Patten Hall, Vilunarleigh-street, on Monday .evening, the Bishop of Liverpool (Dr. Chavasse) hinted at 4:llvisions which may be made in certain parishes in Warrington. Until the war was over, said .his Lordship, they must not expect any church building, but it was as well to know what was wanted so that they might quietly lay their plans and get ready. The time had come when St. Elphin's Parish, with its 20,000 people, must be divided. 'The Rector had looked ahead and had secured, through the generosity of Colonel Ireland Black- burne, a site for a church. And the time had 41so come when St. Paul's Church would have to put out a shoot in place of the mission room An Hoyle-street. The period was not far dis- tant when the unique church of St. Luke's, tiverpool-road, would become a centre and a Parish of its own, and when the church of St. Darnabas, Lovely-lane, would be completed. His Lordship next- had a word to say upon finance, remarking that they had a scheme for the raising of £lO,OOO a year. That sounded a great deal, but if the 66,000 communicants only gave id. a week each the money would be more than realised. Everyone of them should give something, and then if they were in need of a church, or a vicarage, or required the endow- ments of the clergy increasing, a grant would be made if those who had control considered the Schemes sound and good. • Having recommended definite religious tea:th- ing as an aid to the development of character. hie Lordship snoke of the nlace the laity should have in church work. "We clergy must give the laity their proper position" he observed. "We must take them as our counsellors, and I hope the day is coming when every chur.,h in the deanery will have its Church Council, and when every rector and vicar will do nothing Without first consulting that little parliament of laymen who gather around him." (Ap- plause.)

The, -Rector (the Rev. Canqn Willis) presified over the conference, which WAS well attended.

128th ANNIVERSARY.

Cairo Street Sunday School's Long

History.

The 128th anniversary of the Sunday school vas held in Cairo-street Chapel on. Sunday. The preacher, morning and evening, was the Ile, V. W. Whitaker, of Platt Chapel, Manches- ter, and in the afternoon an address was given 07 Mr. Arthur E. Hoyle, of the Secondary School, Warrington. .„In the morning Mr. Whitaker spoke on "The -rriegthood of the Home," describing the in- ilLicence of home in child-life and emphasising 'in need of religion there. In the afternoon Mr. Hoyle gave a bright and manly address on "Dr. Grenfel, of Lab- •:4l:r." Mr. Hoyle described in a very inter- 13g manner how Dr. Grenfel had first of all —rne under the influence of Dr. Moody, the g.reat revivalist, and then had undertaken evangelistic work in the slums of London, cgrrying on there a school for newspaper lads. A.fterwards, at the suggestion of Sir Frederick Treves, Dr. Grenfel organised the Mission to Deep Sea Fishers, which has been so much appreciated; and then, after that, he moved MI to greater work in Labrador. Mr. Hoyle described how, in that inhospitable country, Dr. Grenfel, almost alone, had accomplished great tasks. Along that 1,000 miles of coast- line Dr. Grenfel has established churches, schools, hospitals, trading stations, and has Practically been the sole inspirer of a new civilisation. Mr. Hoyle told the story of Dr. arenfel's courage, bravery and •industry in an irlaPressive and inspiring manner, which could tot but have a good effect on the children Wko heard him.

CHURCH OF ENGLAND MEN'S SOCIETY.

arrington And District Federation Annual Meeting At Lyinm.

On Saturday there took place at Lymm the annual general meeting of the Warrington and District Federation of the C.E.M.S. The whole of the nine branches comprised within the Federation were well represented, and alto- gether from 50 to 60 delegates and members attended.

The preliminary proceedings consistea of a service in the Parish Church, conducted by the Rector (the Rev. G. B. Thurston). Tea, in the Masonic Hall, followed, the Rector's wife acting as hostess. Tasteful floral decorations had been lavishly employed to make the room pleasing and attractive, and the careful kind- ness of the Lymm ladies had placed on each man's plate a button-hole of pansies. The re- freshments disposed of, and suitable acknow- ledgments made, the main business of the assembly commenced on the Rectory lawn, fringed with its delightful sylvan surroundings. The Hon. Secretary and also the Federation Chairman reported on the year's work, dealing more particularly with the co-operation which had been evinced by all concerned, and con- gratulating the Federation on its keen officers and useful work. A balance sheet showing a record amount of receipts and expenditure was unanimously passed., - The following changes in the officers for 1916-17 were then made :—Mr. J. V. Partington '(late Press secretary) becomes chairman, and the present chairman, the Rev. Greenham (Halton Parish), in accordance with rules, automatically becomes vice-chairman. The secretary and assistant retain office. Mr. F. T. Newbold (Sankey Branch) was elected Press secretary. Delegates to the annual Conference at •Manchester in September were elected, St. Paul's, Warrington, and Lymm Branches each sending a member.

WYCLIFFE P.S.A. •

Mr. B. Farrar presided over. Sunday's meet- ing, and the Rev. T. T. James gave the address upon "The Lord's Prayer." Prayer was a good and most necessary act, he said. Jesus lived in the constant spirit of prayer; it was a living claim upon Him. The Apostles saw Him daily, heard His prayers, and felt the inspiration of them. The Christ, if in all His purity, needed prayer and was the stronger for prayer, so we can follow His great and grand example. And, as the Apostles, we can say "Lord teach us how to pray." '

Mr. C. Cotton and Mr. A. Healey took part in the meeting. Mr. Payton rendered an organ solo, which was greatly appreciated.

THE COLLEGE.

Lady Appointed As Vice- Principal.

GUILD ANNUAL MEETING.

Miss F. G. Timewell, who has for many years been on the staff of the Warrington Training College, has been appointed Vice- Principal to succeed the Rev. W. Hendy-Cock, who will shortly take up the duties of assistant diocesan inspector for the North-Western Divi- sion of the Manchester Diocese.

It is in accordance with the requirements of the Board of Education that the new Vice- Principal is a lady, who will reside at the College.

MISS FERRIMAN RETIRES.

College Lady Superintendent's Long

And Faithful Service.

There was an attendance of over 600 members of the Warrington Training College staff, present and past students and friends on the occasion of the annual meeting, last Saturday, of the, St. Katharine's Guild of former students, an occasion which was utilised for making several presentations to Miss Ferriman upon her retirement from the post of Lady Superintendent, Which she has held for many years. There being no room in the College to hold the large assembly the ceremony took place out of doors, on one of the terraces. The Principal (the Rev. Canon Morley • Stevenson), raised on a small platform, made a brief, but very touching speech. He referred in feeling terms to the fact that they had met to do honour to Miss Ferriman and her 41 years' faithful service to the College. He re- minded his hearers that their knowledge of what she had been and done was very limited compared with his. He had received great nelp from her during the whole time they had worked together, and he could not thank her properly for the sympathy and support she had always given him. All felt they would like Miss Ferriman to take some remembrance of the College away with her into her new Sphere of life, in which they hoped she would enjoy a long and pleasant period of rest. A very generous response had been made to the appeal to "old students," and he had much pleasure in handing to her, on their behalf, a very unassuming little book, more interesting inside than it appeared outside. It was a bank- book for a deposit of .RlOl, which they wished her to expend in any way that she pleased. Other gifts were then handed' to Miss Ferri- man—a silver-plated breakfast dish from the Principal and Mrs. Stevenson, a canteen of plate and cutlery from past and present mem- bers of the Staff, a gold watch bracelet from the present Students, and a silver butter-dish and knife from the Manchester Old Students' Association.

Three , hearty cheers were then given at the Principal's request, and Miss Perriman uttered a few words of grateful thanks, the Principal having promised, on her behalf, that as it would be imaossible for them all to hear her, she would niit make a speech, but would say all that she had to say in the next number of the College Magazine.

THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, JUNE 10, 1916.

2,300 WIVES & WIDOWS

Make Garden Party Biggest Held In The Borough.

MAYOR'S THOUGHTFULNESS.

Two little inquisitive and venturesome boys, whose trousers had been sent back to port for repairs times beyond calculation, heard a grand band playing in Bank Park on Saturday afternoon, and, being turned about when they presented themselves at the gate, tried to get inside by contracting themselves through the railings, but a policeman saw and frightened them home.

Had they been lucky enough to effect an entrance they would have attended the largest garden party ever held in Warrington. s Alderman Peter Peacock's mayoralty has been made memorable by many outstanding events. That of Saturday will enrich it, for by his and his wife's kindly act no fewer than 2,300 wives and widows of Warrington soldiers and sailors spent hours which must be reckoned among their happiest since the war began. Husbands in the trenches and on the seas will feel cheered by the news sent them in their letters. One thousand eight hundred had signified their desire to be guests, and to these specially designed invitations were forwarded, but Sat- urday ushered with it such delightful al fresco weather that 500 more came knocking at the gate, and, qualifying by showing their ring papers, were as welcome as the rest. The main theatre of operations was on the lawn behind the Town Hall, though, of course, the whole of the gardens fitted into friendly territory, and were by no means shunned. But the principal venue was the lawn, for there congregated the host and hostess, the band of the 2nd South Lancashire Regiment, the Scarlet Poms, the tea tent (a marquee, which more than suggested a flower show) and the ice cream and lemonade stalls. And all these at- tractions were to be had just for the asking. Selections by the band, under Mr. Cahill, were frequent and generally of a light order, the metre sometimes prompting the ladies to couple up and waltz on the agreeable green sward. Like their husbands, in another sense, they were comrades in arms. On two or three occasions they acted more collectively, a long line of them indulging in a crocodile crawl. As time sped on a decorous walk took them all to the tea tables.

===== 0002601_19160610_art0038.txt | included=true | included =====
ENTERTAINMENT AND TEAS.

Very delightful, too, were the programmes sub- mitted by Mr. Whittle's troupe of Scarlet Ponis, looking far brighter, than seaside pierrots. Songs, humorous and serious, gained their objective, the stories were always funny, a ventriloquial sketch by Mr. Will Morris, of Chester, made us imagine two persons where there was only one, and some conjuiing tricks lifted our eyebrows. Along with the Poms were Madame S. Silcock, Madame B. Eckersley, who rendered some very pleasing songs indeed. Before the guests had been in the,grounds and sun many minutes there was "great business" being done at the ice &cam stall, those with more sensitive tastes taking in lemonade. The Mayor was one of the busiest attendants, serving the biscuit horns with the alacrity of an Italian vendor born to the work.

Mrs. Mitchell was responsible for the catering, Mrs. Brooke garnering forty young ladies to lot as waitresses at tea, which was handed to the guests as they sat on chairs and forms in real afternoon-tea style.

The lawn presented a picturesque and animated scene—picturesque by reason of zhe bunting and allied flags which flew in the warm breeze, and animated because life was being enjoyed to the full. Not merely "wives and widows" were present. Very kindly the Mayor and Mayoress had invited the Town Councillors, and most of these gentlemen found their way to the party with their wives, after- wards enjoying tea in the ,Town Hall, as also did the waitresses and entertainers. There was to have been dancing on the front lawn in the evening, but owing to the naval battle this was wisely ruled out of the pro- gramme.

THE MAYOR-"A JOLLY GOOD FELLOW."

During the afternoon the Mayor addressed the gathering, and at the close three mighty cheers rang through the air for him, followed by "For he's a jolly good fellow." Then more cheers. In -the course of his speech, the Mayor ex- pressed his thankfulness that those present had accepted his invitation. He was exceedingly pleased that the weather had favoured them. He was proud of the way in which the War- rington boys, fathers and husbands had rallied to the country's call—(cheers)—and he thought that his giving their dependants a garden party was some manifestation of his pleasure. (Cheers.) He would have liked to invite all the relatives of the heroes, but the park could not accommodate them' all. His object that day was to give happiness to the wives and widows and make them feel that there were hearts who cared for them and were anxioUs to give them comfort. His hope was that their dear hus- bands would return safe to Warrington and that he should be able to,welcome them. (Ap- plause.) ' He trusted those present would bear up during the period they had got to wait for the return from the Front, and that their best expectations would be realised. At all times the Mayoress and himself were anxious to extend help for their happiness. In conclusion, his Worship thanked Colonel Moggridge for sending the 2nd South Lancashire Regiment Band, the Scarlet Poms for their entertainments, Mrs. Brooke and her lady friends, Mrs. Mitchell, the park officials, and nll others who had lent a hand in making the affair a success. (Cheers.) It should be added that in the centre of the lawn there was a framed roll of honour to the heroic dead. It stood on an easel and, as some of the women pointed out to their friends the names of their late husbands, pathetic scenes were not infrequent.

===== 0002601_19160610_art0039.txt | included=true | included =====
"SWAT THE FLY.."

Grim Reasons Why The Fly

Must Be Killed.

The house fly is beginning to make its appear- ance, and the sooner we begin the campaign against it the better. Beyond all question the fly is a carrier of typhoid, tuberculosis, cholera infantum, cerebro-spinal meningitis, diphtheria, scarlet fever, bronchitis, small-pox and many other diseases, so that the campaign against it is based upon a very real danger. When it comes to multiplication of the species the Australian rabbit is not in it with the fly. Dr. Howard, of the bureau of entomology, at Washington, says that in a climate such as exists there 12 generations of flies are produced in a single summer. As one fly will lay 120 eggs the result, if all of these should hatch and re- produce their kind in like ratio, would be appalling. Their unchecked development in 12 generations would measure 268,778,165,- 861 cubic miles, or considerably more than the total mass of the earth. Fortunately this does not occur or an end would speedily come to the human race. As a matter of actual experience and observation, it is estimated that from each pair of flies surviving the-winter some 8,000,000 living insects are propagated during the sum- mer.

===== 0002601_19160610_art0040.txt | included=true | included =====
A Product Of Filth.

The fly is a product of filth. It breeds in it, luxuriates in it and then when the notion appeals to it enters your home and wipes its disease-laden feet and shakes its bacilli sprinkled wings on your food and drink. The fly that lights just for a, second on the nipple_ of the baby's bottle may mean crepe on the door and a pathetic little coffin in the parlour. It is self- evident that everyone's duty is to swat the fly. The fly has no friends and so far as can be seen has no usefulness in the world. It is not used even for bait, is not kept as a pet, is not under the protection of the Society for the Prevention of Cruelty to Animals. It is a nuisance, a danger, it menace to human life and a perversity to bald heads. Swat the fly!

A Rice Cake. Half a pound of rice, lb. of flour, -lb. of loaf sugar, nine eggs, flavouring, ill,. of butter; separate the 'whites from, the Yolks of the eggs, whisk both well, and add to the yolks the butter beaten to a cream; stir in the flour and rice, and beat the mixture well, then.-addthe whites of the eggs; beat the cakes again for tome time, put into a buttered mould and bake for one hour aid half.

The Revival of Narrow Ribbon Velvet. Early Victorian fashions—modified to suit the times—have inspired this summer's modes, and every week witnesses the revival of an old- world• fashion or some detail of dress or its ornamentation. The almost universal use of ribbon velvet is a case in point. In the broad,:r widths it has for some time been almost universally used on garments of all descrip-. tions, in the form of tucks, and as edgings to the long narrow pockets placed on each side of tailored skirts and dresses. Now the narrow black ribbon velvet has just made its appear- ance as a trimming for dainty summer dresses and blouses. Enormous quantities of it are used on one dress, since as many as twenty rows form one group on a coloured muslin ,skirt, and there are two, sometimes three of I these groups. Frequently, however, they are graduated. The bodice and sleeves are orna- mented in the same way. These rows of narrow ribbon velvet look well on the tulle dresses in delicate tints that are in great favour for young girls' evening frocks.

Old-time Stitchery. Smocking is another revival that is finding much favour as an ornamentation for cotton and other frocks, as Well as for blouses, Old stitches are sought for in books and prints, and workers are fast becoming experts in the most difficult patterns. This form of stitchery is used instead of gathers, to hold the fullness at the waist of some of the more expensive white linen dresses; the sleeves being smocked on the shoulders to match. Materials of all descriptions are smocked just now, but the lighter the fabric, the more difficult the work proves. A good crepe de chine smocks admir- ably, and is in great request for blouses, blouse coats, sports coats, and jumpers, adorned with this effective handiwork. Spotted foulard forms another modish trimming for blouses made of fine white baptiste, collars, cuffs and ties being Made of this silk. Rook Pie and Stone Soup. Rooks, we are told, can be, made into an excel- lent pie. This is no doubt true. But according to some of the recipes published, it, in some respects, very much resembles the famous "stone soup," says "Farm and Home." An adventurer had a wonderful stone from which excellent soup could be made, and in his tramps he made good use of it. The villagers were attracted by his statements and came to see the preparation, He pointed out that though the soup was good when the stone was employed alone, it could be improved in various ways. So a few carrots obtained from one, a few turnips from another, potatoes from a third, and bones from a fourth, were gradually added. And all agreed that the stone soup was excellent. So with the rook pie. When the 'steak and the rest of it are all added, the trouble is to find the rook: It reminds us of the story of the charitable old *lady who gave a few crusts to a poor beggar, with the remark: "If you soak them in a quart of milk, add a pound or so of currants, some sugar and lemon peel, they will make a very nice pudding for you."

Barley Cream. Take 2oz. of pearl barley, boil it in water till it is very tender; then rub it through a hair sieve, and put it to one pint of cream, with an egg well beaten; sweeten it, and let it boil. You may, if you like, leave some of the whole barley in.

By MARJORIE.

Women on the Land.

Before the war, about a million of men were employed on the land, and Of these, more than a quarter have joined the Army. Agriculture has thus been brought to danger point, and the decreased production must-bring about a serious shortage of food in the near future, unless the situation can be saved by the women. A Women's War Agricultural Committee. for Middlesex has been formed, and those who are in a position to work without payment, are asked to offer their services. Altogether thirty- five thousand women have registered, and are more willing to work on the land than the farmers are to engage them. The greater num- ber of these women have had some training, or are accustomed to dairy and other work. Of the sixteen hundred who have registered in Oxfordshire nearly three hundred are already at work, and doing well. Farmers in the North and East are more willing to engage women than are those in the South and West. It is expected that a large amount of holiday labour will be available, and that soldiers will be allowed to give some help %luring the hay and wheat harvests.

Home-made Soap. From a. penny book issued by the Central Committee for National Patriotic Organisations, and Very clearly and concisely written by .the Hon. Mrs. Lionel Guest, it would appear that every family is in a position to make its own soap at practically no cost whatever. The secret lies in the saving of every scrap of fat, which in the wasteful British way, has hitherto been allowed to choke up the kitchen drain. How- ever small, the quantity collected daily, it should be melted and stored in a lard pail; there will be no disagreeable odour if the fat is melted over a gentle heat, and not allowed in smoke or to burn. Six pounds of fat will make 81 pounds of soap, if the simple directions given in this book are followed. In the West of England many families still make their own soap, the preparations being included in the usual routine of the household. Mrs. Guest gives valuable advice on the subject of home laundering, and proves that when the soap :an be made from waste grease, with the addition of a few other ingredients, all inexpensive, a serious item can be eliminated from the weekly expenses of every household. The Shortage of Paper.

Hitherto paper has been so cheap and plenti- ful that few people troubled to be careful about the use of it, but a period of strict economy in this as in other matters is now setting in. Some householders count it an advantage to have their letter boxes comparatively free of handbills, circulars and catalogues, but purchasers and shopkeepers are faced with a drastic scarcity of wrapping materials, and before very long, it may be compulsory for customers to carry home their parcels done up in their own paper. Brown paper and string can be used several times for wrapping purposes if preserved, and tissue paper will not be so lavishly used by milliners and shopkeepers as heretofore, so that every sheet should be preserved. Stationery, like everything else, is costing more, and unused

DAYLIGHT SAVING.

My DEAR CHILDREN,— What an intelligent set of nephews and nieces I have, to be sure! When I asked you to write upon daylight saving, I was in some doubt as to the wisdom of doing so, but the letters I have received show that you are all well informed on "general knowledge" sub- jects. Nearly every writer this week has stated all the points about daylight saving, and I have been unable to reproduce more than short ex- tracts from each, after selecting the winning composition. You will understand that, won't you ? All the letters were good and worth printing, but they were so many, and of course dealt with the same points very largely. So many were equal in value that I had to decide to place that letter first that gave most of the opinions of the Writer. As I have said before, I like to know what children think about things, and to hear them describe their own experiences bear- ing upon a given subject. The best letter judged by this standard was sent by Niece Elsie Johnson (No. 777), 323, Lovely-lane, who attends Wycliffe SchoOl, and won a prize a month or six weeks ago—the last time the prize went to a Wycliffe scholar. The competition this week will be an essay on How I Spent Whit Monday.

There will be no holidays this Whit-week for anybody but school children; so you are highly favoured.

New Members.

My new members continue to increase, some of the names being sent in by the new members by friends. One new niece themselves, some wrote asking could she join and how to do it- They join the Corner. by saying they tvish to do so, 'mid writing their name, address, age and school, just as this little lady did. She will find her name and number in this week's list. Your loving UNCLE JACK.

HIGHLY COMMENDED.

Niece Eeina Fellows, 44, Bewsey-road (Evelyn- street) says that now we. are in such difficulties the people have come to their senses and day- light saving has been brought into force. She describes the many benefits of the Act, and con- cludes: "I am sure that if Mr. Willett were alive he would be very glad to know of the great service that he has rendered to his coun- try."'

Niece Essie Price, 26, Causeway-avenue (Arp- ley-street School), in one of the best letters re- ceived, says that at last England has learnt a bit of common sense! I think most people are in favour of daylight saving, and I expect they will be more so when their gas bill comes in. Niece Isabella Sankey, 99, Kimberley-street (St. Barnabas'), says that people's eyes will be stronger, because, instead of working in gas- light, they will have the beneficial daylight. Nephew Lewis Geddes, 51, Laira-street, thinks people are getting more in favour of it as they see what it means. It saves gas, and is a great thing for cyclists, motorists, bowling men, &c., and it enables people to enjoy a walk in the country in the evening, which must be good for their health.

Niece Edith Pugh, 17, Ship-street, Brecon, who although she appears to have left Manches- ter-road, retains her interest in the Corner, considers daylight saving an excellent thing, as it gives us an extra hour to be out in the fresh morning air, and—a sensible thing—enables us to save the cost of gas and electricity in the evening. It is safer now to travel at night, and the arrangement is especially convenient for shop assistants, who can now take a walk in the broad daylight when they have finished their work.

Nephew Leo Eastens, 156, Winwick-road, a new member, whom I welcome to our Corner, thinks the Act will be very inconvenient to people who are careless. Some people go to theatres and picture palaces, and it is nine o'clock when they come out. By the day it will be eight o'clock. and people will not go to bed until it is dark, and if they have to get up early they may lose several hours' sleep, and they w4ll. lose an hour when they go to work.

BIRTHS, MARRIAGES, DEATHS.

Notices of births, marriages, and deaths, in memoriam notices, and acknowledgments of sympathy are inserted in "The Examiner" at the following rates :—Not exceeding 20 words, 1/6 each; every additional seven words, 6d.

Births. GUEST.—At Lynton Grange, Padgate, on June 7, the wife of Herbert W. Guest, of a son.

Marriages.
