THE EXAMINER,

SATURDAY, JUNE 10, 1916.

===== 0002601_19160610_art0056.txt | included=true | included =====
NO WALKING DAY.

Last week we advocated the postponement of Walking Day because of the interruption it would, if held, entail upon the production of very necessary war material. The MAYOR called a meeting of the school superintendents last night to decide " finally" in the matter. In the meantime the Ministry of Munitions had heard of the contemplated holiday and applied a kindly but firm veto. The only thing the superintendents, therefore, had to decide was the- kind of substitute that should be found for Walking Day. The substitute will take the form of separate field treats for individual schools if the school authorities feel justified in holding them.

===== 0002601_19160610_art0057.txt | included=true | included =====
A NAVY CLOSED FOR REPAIRS.

The way in which the British Government and the German Government have communi- cated the result of the great naval battle in the North Sea will, we hope, enalle neutral countries to arrive at a final judgment as to the credibility of the two nations. All the world, we think, knows upon which side Right exists; all the world, we fear, does not yet realise that Might will inevitably succumb before the onslaught of Right. The scientific lying of the Germans has befogged the for- eigners' intelligence, though Americans and others of the more enlightened type, have realised, as we have realised from the first, that Germany must eventually go down before the tremendous influences, moral and material, that are opposed to her. We have no doubt that ere long we shall have a great opportunity on land, as we have just had on sea, of showing the world that Germany is in a hopeless plight, and that her reputation rests largely upon falsity and slimness. •The sea battle has given the world a rare object lesson. On the one hand, the British Admir- alty issued a report which, if it erred at all, erred on the side of moderation. If we look at the list of our casualties, as they stand re- vealed to-day, we find them exactly what they were stated to be in the first official com- munique, which appeared in last Saturday's "Examiner." If anything, the list is more satisfactory, as several destroyers which were then missing, subsequently reported at their base. But what of our friends the enemy? They precipitately claimed a victory and gave their losses as being next to nothing. In fact, that claim was as precipitate as their flight had been when they were tackled by JELLICOE and his men. Early this week they I were forced to admit that the Elbing had to be added to their losses, and on Thursday came the startling admission not only that the battle cruiser "Lutzow and the light cruiser Rostock had been sunk, but that the authori- ties had kept this news back "for military reasons." They declared at each step that what was revealed represented, their total losses. From what we know there will be further postscripts to be added to the truth as the days go on. But we have circumstan- tial evidence, even stronger, of the fact that the Germans have found that the sea is not quite their element. The mist allowed them to escape to Wilhelmshaven, and that place has now been closed even to the German public. A victorious fleet should surely have been bedecked and thrown open to the inspec- tion of an admiring people who had at length gained their place on the Sea, as .they had already gained their place in the Sun. As a matter of fact, they have been Warned off the sea as they have been ejected from the places where the sun shone upon them. Wilhelms- haven is clearly closed for repairs. It is true the Germans clumsily claim that the fact that they have rescued some of our sailers from the scene of the battle proves that they remained in possession at the close. Against that we have the fact that the German fleet did ko home; that the British Fleet proudly rides :IP sea at leisure; that our merchant ships and those of our Allies have the freedom of the seas, while all German ships are still rot- ting in the harbours where they have been refugees since the war began. If this is "The Day," and the Germans are satisfied with it, Englishmen are not discontent.

===== 0002601_19160610_art0058.txt | included=true | included =====
LORD KITCHENER.

The death of LORD KITCHENER affected this country in much the same way, we imagine, as the death of NELSON affected :our fore- fathers. Both fell in the hour of victory, when the.nation was longing to honour them and acknowledge the debt it owed them. Both had done their work; and the nation .was robbed of the privilege -of doing in life what it will always do in death. That KITCHEN-ER will live for all time as one of the world's

I hope the Padgate workers will .see fit to enter the scheme. which is proving a success generally. Mr. George Mather has promised to use his influence. He is a weighty gentleman and should succeed.

That inhabitants of the rural area should "adopt" Lancashire prisoners of war was also suggested, and Mr. Newcombe at Bewsey Chambers, will be pleased to hear from those whom generosity inclines to lend assistance to our worthy men in this way. Warrington itself is taking a great interest in the prisoners.

At the Appeal Tribunal at Liverpool on Thursday a Warrington appellant mentioned that among other things he made coffins. "We don't want coffins nowadays," observed the Lord Mayor, probably thinking of crema- torium urns: A few minutes later his Lordship invited his colleagues to a tea urn in his parlour.

During the hearing his Lordship expressed his surprise that there is not a public abattoir in Warrington. "I thought you had every- thing in Warrington," he said. "Mr. Peacock (who was sitting next to him) thinks a great deal of the place." Our Mayor is never back- ward in putting Warrington forward. I dare- say he has already told the Lord Mayor about ,how Liverpool used to be known as "Liverpool, near Warrington."

A great deal of disappointment was caused last Saturday afternoon by the announcement that Mr. H. Pollen, the famous naval expert, would not lecture.

In the circumstances one could quite under- stand him cancelling this engagement, as his brother, Father Pollen, who is a Roman Catholic Chaplain on board a warship, had been seriously wounded, and he went to visit the hospital in- stead of coming here. It is likely, however, that Mr. Pollen will visit Warrington in the near future.

One of the Rev. G. Vale. Owen's warrior corfe- spondents reflects upon the state of Warrington now—" not many men about, which would give you some idea what it would be like to be the only man on earth."

If that correspondent could be spirited to Sankey-street some Sunday evening and see the number,of " men " who have nothing better to do with themselves in these awful times than idle about and block np the entrances to shops, he would have "some idea of what it i. like" to have a surplus of " men " of a kind.

He might also have something to say about the parents who allow the young girls and yount; women to run riot as they do—for he is evidently of a reflective .turn of mind.

"Birds, in proportion to their weight; prob- ably eat more than any other living thing." I read this in an American paper this week and, remembering the havoc the sparrows have wrought among my lettuce, the seed put down to cure bald patches in my lawn and other things too numerous to mention, as the auctioneers say in their advertisements, I have come to the conclusion that our American cousins have at length taken to speaking the truth.

THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, JUNE 10, 1916.

heroes is certain. Before this war began he had done service to mankind that would have placed him in the rank of great men. When the war began we instinctively turned to him and we did not turn to him in vain. He saw from the first the gigantic proportions the struggle would assume, and he set himself out to raise an army which grew to five and six millions simply by his invitation. In a dark hour he reminded us of our staying power, and we have justified his confidence. Whatever the Germans may do, KITCHENER'S work for their undoing is completed. He has organised success, and we must needs now win with his work for a foundation. We mourn his loss where we should have been de-

lighted to hail his triumph; and the debt we owe him can only be enhanced by the depriva- tion which the mischances of war have occa- sioned us.

GOSSIP.

"Touch a sailor for luck" is a favourite omen. with young ladies.

When one of our local heroes of the Jutland fight was passing along Winwick-street yester- day a young lady put the maxim into practice and touched him on the shoulder.

The sailor boy didn't even turn a hair. He -old me he was quite accustomed to it.

Last week SOO Warrington Women Liberals were presented with Government war workers' badges, and I have since learned that similar emblems of patriotic endeavour are also being worn by 120 ladies in Penketh and Sankey.

There are, however, other assiduous ladies in the rural district—notably at Padgate—who have not received the badges to which they would be entitled if they were to affiliate with the Lancashire County War Comforts' Asso- ciation, which has its offices in Peter-street, Manchester. .

That they should join was advocated ,by W. Furness and Mr. B. S. Newcombe at the monthly meeting of the Rural District Council on Wednesday, the main reason being that the production of certain species of comforts would then be systematised, and the cost of sending them would be cheapened by reason of the fact that the goods may te dispatched free of carriage.. -

===== 0002601_19160610_art0061.txt | included=true | included =====
THEATRES AND KINEMAS.

What Is Coming Next Week.

" Pals " has proved a considerable attraction at the Royal Court Theatre this Week. Next week we are to have a first-class revue entitled "All Eyes !" with the celebrated Fred Kitchen at the head of affairs. This is the first piece booked by the new manager, Mr. Harry War- burton, and we may be sure he will start well. He has some excellent shows on his lists and, with the support which should be given, we can rely onfhim "staying the course." Although Jack Lane has been unable to appear owing to being in a motor accident, the pro- gramme is a splendid one at the Palace an 4 Hippodrome. The singing of Misses Mabel Braine and Jessie Reeve is of a high quality; and we see great possibilities in Miss Susie Marney as a comedienne. Next week those suc- cessful pantomimists, George, Willie, West and McGinty are to top the bill with "Jerry and Co.—Builders?" The Peerless Potters are com- ing with some sensational aerial feats. Le Max, 'an Australian juggler and equilibrist, and Frank IH. Fox, a character comedian, are among the others in the programme. Splendid pictures have been shown at the Star Kinema. and during the first half of next 1 week we are to have "The Jockey of Death "7 a sensational film. The great "Bella Donna is to be presented in the second half. This is, indeed, a wonderful picture of a wonderful book. There was an enjoyable though circum- scribed edition of it presented at the Royal Court Theatre a few weeks ago, and those whose opinions were charmed by it then are sure to have them enhanced by a view of the picture. Pauline Frederick is being featured as Bella Donna, and the scenes were actually taken in Egypt. The main pictures at the Picture House this week have been "His Wife's Past" and "Man and a Woman." Those announced for next week include "A Queen's Love" and "Woman's Weakness." Both sound interesting, and should prove attractive. The first episode of "The Diamond From the sky "—the picture-story which is now running in "The Examiner "—has proved a big attrac- tion at the Grand Electric Theatre, Bridge Foot, and the Pavilion, Sankey Green. It will be continued next week, and may be anticipated by a perusal of the story in these pages. At both of these houses next week interesting pro- grammes will be submitted, that at the Grand including the famous laughter-makers, Sidney Drew and Charlie Chaplin; that at the Pavilion including "The Marl .of the Hour" and "The Prisoner of Zenda.".

TERRITORIALS DECORATED.

Last Friday a supplement of the "London Gazette" was published containing a list of awards to officers and men of the British Army for war services, on the occasion Of the King's birthday. The list contained some 3,880 names. Sixty-four appointments and promotions were made in the Order of the Bath, and 225 in the Order of St. Michael and St. George, while the

The following local names were in the list :

D.S.O.

Major(Temporary Lieutenant-Colonel) Harold T. Cotton, 2nd South Lancashire Regiment. Recently stationed at Orfopd Barracks. Assumed command of the battalion at the Front last autumn.

Major L. W. Herbert, South Lancashire Special Reserve.

Major W. N. Pilkington, South Lancashire Regiment (T.F.). The fifth son of the late Colonel Richard Pilkington. Has served with the regiment since mobilisation under his eldest brother, Colonel Lionel Pilkington, the officer commanding the sth South Lancashire Regiment.

number of promotions gazetted was 274. The D.S.O. was awarded to 373 officers and the Military Cross to 708 officers, including the Prince of Wales. The awards to the rank and file were 597 Distinguished Conduct -Meals and 1,217 Military Medals. In addition 514 mem- bers of the nursing services received the Royal Red Cross Decoration.

MILITARY MEDAL.

Three Territorials Receive This Honour.

Private J. Clarke, First 4th South Lancashire Regiment (T.F.). Private T. Dodd, 2nd South Lancashire Regi- ment.

Private W. Hull, 2nd South Lancashire Regi- ment.

Pr:vate J. Pickavanca, 2nd South Lancashire Regiment. Sergeant J. E. Webb, First 4th South Lanca- shire Regiment (T.F.). Sergeant T. Widd, First 4th South Lancashire Regiment (T.F.).

PRIVATE JOS. CLARKE,

whose wife and one child reside at 134, Gorsly- lane, is the son of Mr. John Clarke, 59, Slater- street, Latchford. Father and son work for Messrs. Jos. Crosfield and Sons. Private Clarke had served his four years in the Territorirds, but he immediately rejoined on the outbreak of war. His conduct, especially in the charge on June 16th last year, was praiseworthy, and was brought to the notice of the Brigade Command9r. He was mentioned in dispatches among twelve local Territorials thus honoured, and reported in "The Examiner" on New Year's Day.

SERGEANT JAMES EDWARD WEBB, who was wounded in February, and is now in a Blackpool Convalescent Camp, is 28 years of age, and the son of Mr. and Mrs. J. E. Webb, 25, Oldham-street, Latchford. He was called up with the Territorials on the outbreak of the war. He is a St. Ann's School "old boy," and an employe of Messrs. Jos. Crosfield and Sons. He was an Association football player, being captain of the Latchford F.C. At onetime he ,wfts a member of the Lymm P.C., and had also played for Altrinchani. •He went in for boxing. Two other brothers are serving in H.M. Forces. These are Sapper George Webb, Royal Engi- leers, and Driver Percy Webb, of the Army Service Corps, an officer's servant.

MILITARY CROSS.

Quartermaster and Hon. Captain Sidney T. Boast, South Lancashire Regiment. Captain Boast, who is 48 years-of age, is well known in Warrington for the manner in which be .keeps us informid -of tli doings of the South Lan- caShires in France, 'en the lighter side of their life, of course. _He Was stationed at Orford Barracks for thirteen years. He joined the Ist Battalion of the South Lancashire Regi- ment es a private, and served in the •South _African War with the 3rd -Battalion as a quartermaster-sergeant. • He obtained the Queen's Medal with three clasps, and also the medal for distingitished conduct in the field. He received his commission on April 22nd, 1911, and since that time has been connected with the 2nd Battalion of the regiment. He has two sons in the -Army. One of them, Corporal S. W. Boast, has been wounded. The other son joined the 3rd Battalion of the South Lanca- shire Regiment. Lieutenant (Temporarx Captain) J. Boast, South Lancashire Regiment, was stationed at Orford Barracks as instructor after his return from India in 1910. He also went through the South African War and took a creditable part in several important engagements. Be was present at the relief of Ladysmith. Temporary Second-Lieutenant J. DISTINGUISHED CONDUCT MEDAL.

Staff Q.M.S. J. Whitehouse, A.S.C. Pioneer Sergeant A. G. F. Ackary, 2nd South Lancashire Regiment. _ _ _

Company-Sergeant-Major A. Alldridge, 2nd South Lancashire Regiment._ _

Sergeant W. Ness, First 4th South Lancashire Regiment (T.F., Newton Company). Company-Sergeant-Major S. Nicholson, 2nd South Lancashire Regiment. Regimental Quartermaster-Sergeant G. Red- dock, 2nd South Lancashire Regiment. Company-Sergeant-Major W. A: Rice, 2nd South Lancashire Regiment.

STAFF-SERGT. J. WHITEHOUSE, D.C.M. "For continuous devotion to duty throughout. He has on many occasions carried on the heavy work of the administrative branch office single- handed, with conspicuous ability."

For such work has Warrant Officer J. White- house, a well-known Warrington teacher, been awarded the D.C.M. His home address is 27, Fairfield-road, Stockton Heath. He is the son of Mr. and Mrs. Ted Whitehouse, is married, and has one child.

Enlisting in the A.S.C. on January 24th, 1915, he was with the first landing at Gallipoli, where he served throughout the campaign. After re- cuperating for a time at Suez, his division was sent .to France, from which theatre of war he obtained his first leave a few weeks ago. Staff-Sergeant-Major Whitehouse, who before enlisting was engaged as a teacher at the Bolton Council School, Latchford, was educated at Arpley-street School, and the Boteler Gram- mar School. He began his teaching career -.t Arpley-street School, and afterwards proceeded to Borough-road College, London. He has been teaching at the Bolton Council School for about five years.

===== 0002601_19160610_art0064.txt | included=true | included =====
MARKET INSPECTOR.

A Rise In Salary Pending Re- Arrangement Of Duties.

Councillor Chantler, in moving the adoption of the Markets Committee minutes at the meeting of the Town Council on Tuesday night, said they contained no reference to the late Mr. Place. They had had the• matter be- fore them, however, and intended to go into details as •regarded the position. Mr. Place had been a faithful servant and an upright official of the Corporation. - Councillor Dickenson 'seconded.

Councillor Wilson said he was rather sur- prised the Chairman of the Markets Commit- tee did not touch upon an item in the private agenda on this subject. The resolution passed by the Markets Com- mittee and recorded in the Private agenda stated :

, "That in anticipation of the re-arrangement of the duties of Market Inspector and Inspector of Weights and Measures, and in consideration of the extra duties imposed upon him, ,the salary of Mr. W. H. "QV allin g to n be increased by the sum of £5O per annum, as and from the let May, 1916." Councillor Wilson's Protest. .

He did not know, said Councillor Wilson, whether steps were being taken to fill up the vacant market inspectorship appointment; but he thought there had been undue haste in bringing forward this advance of £5O a year in salary. The whole country had been asked to economise and a few weeks ago they told Cor- poration employes they could not see their way to give a shilling a week rise. In these cumstances he objected to cir- so large a rise, al- though he would agree to £25. Without mean-

ing any disrespect to Mr. Wallington, he moved that this recommendation be sent back. He believed Mr. Wallington to be an honest, honourable and good official; but when they were all preaching economy he objected to this suggestion: it seemed a little behind-the-door way of doing it to put the business on the private agenda. "I am told," said Councillor Wilson, after an interruption, "that he has been doing the work for six months. Perhaps he has. If 60, the committee should have made him a grant. This is an advance that goes on for ever. Councillor Wilson had no seconder. A Mild Scene.

The Mayor said the question had been con- sidered. They were all very sorry indeed to lose Mr. Place and that his son died in the seriice of the country on the day his father was buried. On behalf of the town he had sent his sympathies to the family on their double bereavement. As regarded the other matter Mr. Wallington had done the work for ,twelve months and the amount proposed was not too much. The business was placed on the private agenda So as not to hurt the feelings of the relatives of Mr. Place, who was expected to live some considerable time. Councillor Wilson: From now henceforth I shall be no party (Cries of "Chair, chair.") I am going to say it—(" You are out of order.") It is all right, but lam going to be no party—(The Mayor's bell and cries of "Order.") You will give 5..50 to one— (" Order" and "Chair ")—and I will be no party Councillor Wilson •finished amid much inter- ruption by, the members and the Mayor's bell. He could not be heard, but he was understood to say that for the future he would be no party to the refusal of a rise in wages to the Poorer sections of the workmen employed by the Corporation.

SERGEANT TOM WIDD,

son of Mr. Thomas Widd, of the Roan Cow Inn, Bridge Foot, is one of the old Volunteers, and went out in February with the Warrington Territorials. He is ,twenty-five years of age, and has been associated with the Volunteers and Territorials for .a matter of nearly ten years". He, like his comrade-in-arms, Private Webb, re- ceived .a card of commendation for coolness and bravery in the field, and was mentioned in dis- patches on New Year's Day. He is a keen footbalil player, haying at one time been a mem- ber of a Newton-le-Willows team.

===== 0002601_19160610_art0066.txt | included=true | included =====
PROMOTIONS FOR SERVICE IN THE FIELD.

Under the above heading the " Gazette " an- nounced that the King had been pleased to approve of certain rewards for distinguished ser- vice in the field; and classified with those who were "to be granted the next higher rate of pay under Article 241 of the Royal Warrant" was

Quartermaster and Hon. Lieutenant G. H Ingram, First 4th South Lancashire Regiment.

QUARTERMASTER INGRAM,

who was mentioned in dispatches in January, along with other Warrington Territorials, joined the Rifle Brigade at Aldershot in 1883, and since then he has seen service in many parts of the British Empire, including India, Egypt and South Africa. Just before the outbreak of the South African War he was transferred to the 3rd South Lancashire Regiment, joining the staff of the Ist Volunteers (as it was then) Battalion, and in 1902 he was appointed In- structor to the Mounted Infantry. He succeeded Sergeant-Major Rawlinson in 1905 as Battalion- Sergeant-Major, and in that capacity he gave such a good account of himself that he was promoted some time afterwards to the rank of Quartermaster in the place of Major A. F. G. Potter, who had resigned. •

Infectious Disease.

_ Last week the following new cases of infec- tious disease were notified in Warrington : Scarlet fever, 5; diphtheria, 1; tuberculous phthisis, 5; measles, 11; puerperal fever, 1; erysipelas, 2; ophthalmia, 1. There remained in the hospital :—Scarlet fever patients, 37; enteric fever, 1; diphtheria, 5; phthisis, 23; other diseases, 1; total, 67.. Births More Than Double The Deaths. During the week ended June 3rd there were in Warrington 41 birtbs and 19 deaths. In the corresponding week last year the births were 39 and the deaths 25. .

The deaths resulted from the following causes :—Pneumonia, 3; measles, phthisis, can- cer and heart disease, 2 each; whooping cough, influenza, premature birth, 1 each; all other causes, 5.

VERDUN WHITSUNTIDE.

Crisis In The Grim Struggle Vhere All Must Help. Dr. Macnamara, speaking at Birmingham, pointed out that the German working man is doing double time, therefore it is only fair to the British fighting man that his civilian supporters should do their share. This Whitsuntide every stroke of work done is helping the French to hold their line and ours. Shortage of munitions on sea or land might mean disaster, so that it is a grim struggle in which every civilian as well as every soldier should bear his part. At Pentecost the tongues of flame descended on the Apostles, as to-day fiery inspiration comes to every man and woman to do their best. It is a tremendous opportunity, and every future Whitsuntide the workers will be able to look back with pride on the tireless energy of the Verdun Whit-week. And they can help still more by investing their unspent money in War Savings Certificates, and so paying for the war. The Mayor announced at the Council meet- ing on Tuesday night that in response to a request from the tradesmen's representatives, he wished publicly to state that Whit Monday would not be a holiday of any kind. All shops and business premises ought to be open as usual. No opening of shops would be permitted on Thursday afternoon of this week, even if a tradesman wished to close on Whit Monday.

Whist Drive Postponed. The whist drive in aid of the Thelwall Heys Hospital, arranged to take place on Whit Mon- day, has been postponed until next Saturday at 3.30 p.m.

Citizens' Temperance League. An open-air meeting in connection with the Citizens' Temperance League will be held oa Friday evening, in Bewsey-street. Mr. 3.- Griffiths will preside, and the speaker will be the Rev. T. T. James, president of the League.

Thelwall Heys Patients. The following patients were admitted into the Thelwall Heys Convalescent Hospital on Thurs- day :—Private A. Ward, 21st Manchesters; Private R. Wyatt, Anti-Aircraft Battalioa; Rifleman H. Davies, Rifle Brigade; Lance - Corporal W. Kerrigan, 3rd King's (Liverpool) Regiment; Lance-Corporal G. Preston, South City of London Yeomanry (Rough Riders); Private W. Longhurst, South Middlesex Regi- ment; Rifleman A. Sherwin, K.R.R.• Driver 11- Aspinall, R.F.A.; Lance-Corporal W. A. Had- dock, R.E.; Drummer F. Thomson, R.G.A. Coffee Unadulterated.

The Deputy Mayor (Dr. Joseph) in seconding the adoption of the minutes of the Health Com- mittee at the Town Council meeting on Tues- day, referred to the annual report of the Public Analyst for the year ended December 31st. There was one point, he said, in that report upofl which he looked with a great deal of satisfac- tion. A year or two ago he alluded to the fact that there was a good deal of adulteration of coffee with chicory in Warrington. In some cases it was 51 per cent.; but in the present report adulteration was altogether absent. He thought people should be encouraged when they deserved it as well as reproached, and he had pleasure in making this acknowledgment in respect of the past year. (Hear, hear.)

===== 0002601_19160610_art0073.txt | included=true | included =====
Rev. E. J. Gawne at Warrington.

The Vey. E. J. Gawne, of Liverpool, and s• former minister of the Warrington Circuit, was the special preacher at the Bold-street Wes- leyan School anniversary services held on Sun- day. The services were in every way a success, the collections amounting to £4O 15s. 10d. •

Both morning and evening the Rev. E. J. Gawne had a thoughtful and encouraging word for the children. A flower service was held in the afternoon, over which Mr. John Griffiths presided. Councillor Robert Henshall delivered an interesting address to the children on "Wisdom," whilst a letter was read from Mr. Gerard Doncaster (one of the Sunday School scholars), who is serving with the Forces. The writer expressed his best wishes for a happY and successful anniversary. The teachers and scholars walked in procession during the sing- ing of a hymn, carrying flowers, which were afterwards arranged in front of the pulpit. The scholars and choir rendered special music throughout the day, the soloists being Miss B. Taylor and Miss Muriel Mills.

Reception to Rev. F. L. and Mrs. Grosvenor.

A large and representative gathering of the congregation welcomed the Rev. F. L. Grosvenor and his wife in Emmanuel Church Institute on Thursday evening. The Free Church Council voiced its greetings in the person of the Rev. A. Chessum, who welcomed Mr. Grosvenor to the work "in a town of much difficulty." The greetings of Elmwood Avenue Congregational Church and Wycliffe Church were conveyed in a happy manner bY the Rev. C. P. Pitt.

Letters of regret for non-attendance were re- ceived from Mr. Cole, of Lymm, and Mr. IL Gandy, of Earlestown.

The musical part of the programme was very ably sustained by members of the choir, while Mr. Alfred Dutton, of Lovely-lane, entertained the audience by a character sketch, "Lasca, of Texas and the Rio Grande," and upon being, recalled he gave a very fine impersonation of Uriah neap. Later in the evening he gave an- other character sketch, "0 memory," and, in response to successive recalls, "Ef you like" and "The Student."

During the refreshment period Mr. and Mrs. Grosvenor met the various members of the congregation, to whom the reverend gentleman afterwards expressed his thanks and the earn- est hope that the work of the Church would be built up on the sure Rock of Christ, and strengthened by a prayer-circle. A happy and successful evening closed with votes of thanks to the workers, followed by the singing of the Doxology and the National Anthem. The reverend gentleman preached his first sermon at Emmanuel Church last Sunday.

Citizens' Temperance League. The annual meeting of the Warrington and District Citizens' Temperance League was recently held, the Rev. T. T. James presiding. The treasurer's report was presented by Mr. F. Gandy. Mr. B. T. Parkes gave the secretary's report, which told of the work of the past year, which was very encouraging. Pledges had been signed at all the open-air meetings of 1915. They had also taken part in collecting evidence for the inquiry held at Manchester by the Control Board, after which the town and district were declared part of the area controlled under the Defence of the Realm Act, and the hours of sale of intoxicants restricted. It was decided to for- ward a letter of appreciation of their efforts to the Control Board. The following were elected officers for 1916: President, the Rev. T. T. James; vice-presi- dents, Messrs. J. G. Withinshaw, J. Griffiths, T. Dale and W. J. Walker; treasurer, Mr. F. Gandy; secretary, Mr. B. T. Parkes; assistant, Mr. J. Simpson. , An open-air committee was appointed to arrange another campaign. They have since met and have decided to commence on Friday, June 16th, at Bewsey-street.

HEROES WHO NI ED G I RMANY'S SEA AMBITIONS.

ONE OF BEATTY'S MEN.

Home For His Sister's Wedding

To-day.

TORPEDO MAN ON WARSPITE.

One of Beatty's men touched the knocker at hie home, 27, Farrell-street, Warrington, 'about 7 o'clock on Thursday morning, and a young lardy got out of bed in a hurry to see whether the postgirl had brought a letter from the sea. It was not a letter packet, but a blue jacket

ABLE SEAMAN LIVESEY.

—her young brother, Able Seaman Frank Live-

Bey (20), of H.M.S. Warspite, which did valiant work in the great battle, either sinking or damaging no fewer than three of her opponents —and there was all the ecstatic excitement of a surprise meeting. When an "Examiner" interviewer called Yesterday he had just returned from a tour of the workshops of Messrs. R. A. Naylor, Ltd., Where he had been "lionised " by his former workmates.

Ile was as cheerful as the rest of our sailors. " We scattered them all right," he said, refer- ring to the German ships, "although it took some hard work to keep our end up while we had inferior numbers. The Warspite was one of the first to go at the Germans, and our gun- ners gave of their best. We hadn't been long on the scene before the Indefat.' (Livesey con- tracted the name of the Indefatigable, but he Spoke with affection) went down. That was a set-back, and we ourselves were hit with several shells:—the officers say we were the target of five ships--but we kept going and can vow that we either sank or seriously damaged three of their biggest boats. The noise of battle was terrific— One continuous thunder, the ship fairly shaking with her own discharges and when she was hit. But every one of us had nerved himself to the task. When told we were about to engage the eneraY we went about our duties as if it were an ordinary battle .practice, excepting that we felt 721C)re eager. There was no fear. The officers were calm and calculating; the men quick to .aotion. None of us ever gave a thought that we might be blown up. Being busy and having Your blood up takes your mind off that kind of thing., It is only when the excitement is over and you see what narrow shaves you had that you think of that.

- -

son who, in reply, wrote, "My Dear Ma,—' was greatly shocked to receive your telegram and exceedingly sorry to think that I am unable to go to you, as you are worthy of all the comfort which your sons and daughters can give you at such a terrible time. May God give you health and strength to bear the trial which is upon you. .. . It is, however, impossible by reasqn of distance and pressure of work, due at the end of the month, for me to be with you at the funeral."

Soon after writing that letter Mr. Place's ship steamed out to battle, and he was one of the victims of shell fire. That his death should follow so soon after that of his father is a fact which will gain the deepest sympathy for those of the family who are left: There is also the additional sad recollection that it was only in January last that a sister passed away. Mr. Place was 41 years of age, and had held the high position of Fleet Paymaster for three or four years. As a boy he showed promise of the success he achieved in his manhood, for while at Wycliffe School he won a scholarship for the Grammar School for three years, display- ing such ability that, through the influence of the Earl of Latham, the award was extended a year.

===== 0002601_19160610_art0076.txt | included=true | included =====
FIGHT WITH A SUBMARINE.

"For the most part I was down below in a submerged torpedo discharger, but from this position we were unable to \ act against the enemy for an unfortunate reason, the range tower above us being blown away just as we were about to fire a succession of torpedoes. When that happened we weren't half vexed, but we found other useful work elsewhere. "On our way back to port a German sub- raarine rose to the surface within a few yards of us. It was noticed at once. I was on the liPper deck at the time and saw it fire two tnrpedoes right across our stern. It was too close for us to depress our six-inch guns on to it, so from a top turret we fired a three- Pounder at the periscope and it disappeared. Whether it sank or not We do not know for certain, but we saw plenty of fuel rise to the surface, and, if you are an optimist, you may conclude something bad had happened to it. From then home, at any rate, we were troubled no more. It is an interesting coincidence that Frank Livesey's "leave" will enable him to attend the wedding of his sister, Ada, this afternoon at Bold-street Wesleyan Church.

Another Warrington man on the Warspite was Able Seaman William Bibby, who resides With his parents at 34, Haydock-street. He came home on leave with Seaman Livesey.

SEAMAN PILLING'S STORY.

Eye Witness 01 The Damage Done To

The Enemy.

Certainly the men of the navy who dealt with the German fleet last week stand in no doubt as to the real result of the conflict.

One of our reporters was reminded very forcibly of the truth of this statement when in conversation with one of our local naval heroes, Seaman Robert Pilling, of 49, Laira-street, who is enjoying a few days' leave. He is one of the crew of the vessel on which Paymaster Place met his death, which played an important part 411 the titanic struggle. He was very emphatic an his declaration that the German Navy had auffered such a blow that its power as a fighting -force has been considerably weakened.

Pilling had the mortification of seeing Pay- master Place killed. At the time the officer was 'engaged in conversation with the Chaplain, When he was struck in the back and killed in- stantly. The Chaplain was also hit on the head, tind he succumbed to his injuries shortly after- wards. Seaman Pilling spoke of the high regard and esteem the men on the ship held for the deceased Paymaster. In his own words, he Was °he of the "most popular officers aboard." Though the name of this ship does not appear in the official returns of the North Sea battle she, according to the story of Seaman Pilling, did her "bit." They had been in action about 'three hours when the German High Sea Fleet Put in an appearance. .It was a particularly hot time, and the ship was struck with shells both fore and aft. Our gunners were by n..) leans idle, and they claim to have sunk in all ten boats. He was quite emphatic about the Sinking of the Derflinger and also the damage done to the famous Hindenburg. Whatever doubt Other naval men had about the presence of a FaPpelin he had none. His eyes did not deceive It was hit by one of the other ships and appeared to descend into the sea. He would not RO so far as to say it was sunk. It might have °llly been temporarily damaged, but the gunners certainly registered a hit. When our Grand Fleet came on. the scene the *oelny sent a flotilla of destroyers across their own line and they emitted clouds of smoke, which assisted their fleet in their scamper homewards. litis ship joined in the chase even so far as their 2,wri minefields. Afterwards, for several hours, cY scoured the seas, but failed to find any "race of the enemy or disabled ships. t., Before 'joining the Navy in May of last year, „Taman Pilling was employed at the works of Whitecross Company, and previous to that _Traii engaged at the hairdresser's shop of Mr. red Shaw, Whivrick•etzeet--

===== 0002601_19160610_art0079.txt | included=true | included =====
THE DEATH OF LORD KITCHENER,

WARRINGTON'S SORROW.

Expressed At The Meeting Of The

Town Council.

His Worship the Mayor (Alderman Peter Peacock) at the opening of the Town Council on Tuesday night, said: We are meeting this evening under the shadow of a great national disaster in the death of our honoured Lord Kitchener. I don't think that any words of mine would be sufficient to express our real sorrow at such a national loss as this; no words from anyone could express how sincerely we feel and how the nation must miss such a great and honoured soldier. I will, in the name of War- rington, send to the proper quarter a message expressing our sorrow. I ask you, ,with these few words, to rise and silently honour one of the greatest Englishmen who ever, lived. The members of the Council stood and passed the resolution in silence.

• Flags were raised at half mast on all the public and semi-public buildings of the town, and at most of the schools.

"Hear, hears" were general at the Trades and Labour Council on Tuesday night, when the Chairman (Mr. E. Plinston) referred to the death of Lord Kitchener, and remarked that they would all agree that his Lordship had ren- dered faithful service to his country, and what- ever their opinions on the war might be, his tragic death was greatly deplored.

===== 0002601_19160610_art0080.txt | included=true | included =====
TWO MEMORIAL SERVICES.

Although, no doubt, there will be pulpit re- ferences at all places of worship to the lament- able death of Lord Kitchener, only two special memorial services are announced—at St Paul's Church and Cairo-street Chapel in the morn- ing at 10.30. At the former church the collec- tions will be devoted to the Blinded Soldiers' Fund.

REV. GILBERT HARDING.

Gets A Commission And An Army Chaplaincy.

We are sure that the many friends of Mr. Harding in Trinity congregation will wish to offer to him their warmest congratulations on his well-deserved promotion. Joining the R.A.lai.C. as a private a little over twelve months ago, he was quickly made sergeant, and has now been offered a commission and appointed like- wise an army chaplain. Knowing as we do how efficiently his clerical duties have always been discharged when helping the Vicar with the ser- vices, it is very gratifying that his merits have been recognised in this way.—" Holy Trinity Magazine."

===== 0002601_19160610_art0082.txt | included=true | included =====
TERRITORIALS AT BOWLS.

An interesting bowling match has been played at Armoured Train (of which a party of South Lancashire Territorials form the escort) and a Norfolk (Cyclist) battalion. The " train " team was reinforced, by several members of the R.N.A.S. (Anti-aircraft Section). The Lanca- shire boys were handicapped by playing on a " flat " green, and also by the methods adopted in Norfolk of playing in seta of three men, each set to play each one of its opponents' sets in games of seven up. Despite these disadvantages, the Lancashire lads won by nine sets to seven, and on points by 86 to 74. The Vicar of the parish placed his green at the disposal of the contestants, and also entertained the teams to a very substantial tea. It ia hoped that other matches will be arranged during the summer.

SOLDIERS' FANCY WARE.

Interesting Exhibition And Sale At

Raddon Court.

The special .feature of Pound Day, held at Raddon Court on Thursday, was the wonderful dis- play of work for exhibition and sale,done by the patients. Generously working for their hospital and stimulated by the competition for prizes, the workers had produced artistic and useful articles, which were greatly admired by alt. Dainty embroideries of all description testified to the artistic taste of our soldiers, whilst use- ful door mats, knitted jerseys, gloves, &c., were also on view, and every one was asked to make a beautiful or useful purchase. • ,Perhaps special mention should be made of the delicate and elaborate embroidery of Private Edwards, East Surrey Regiment; the original patriotic designs beautifully carried out by r Private McLeod, King's (Liverpool) Regiment; the exquisitely finished embroidery of Private Lovell, of the Middlesex Regiment; and the paintings sand drawings of Sergeant Smalley, of the Seaforth Highlanders. The wort, was judged by Mrs. Segal- Owen and Mrs. Fox, and at their special request Mrs. Broadbent presented the prizes. Mrs. Fox proposed and Mrs. Segar Owen seconded a vote of thanks to Mrs. Broadbent, remarking that her home had become the happy centre of Red Cross work in this district.

A vote of thanks was also passed to the two ladies who had so kindly acted as judges, and to the artistes who had given a very attractive concert. These were Mrs. McNaught, Miss Longdin, Miss M. Reynolds and Miss Grocott, _ _ _ _

Privates McLeod Eind Wilson were awarded equal numbers of votes by the inmates of the hospital for the best work in the exhibition.

TRIBUNAL CASES FROM STOCKTON HEATH.

Mr. C. E. Linaker, sen., presided at a meeting of the Runcorn Rural Tribunal at the Work- house, Dutton, yesterday. 30,000 Tons of Timber.

Mr. H. N. Linaker, solicitor, represented an employer who asked for exemption of a Stock- ton Heath timber feller. The employer, Mr. Linaker said, had several very large contracts for timber, including one of 30,000 tons. The man had had experience of timber felling in Canada. He was formerly a builder in War- rington. The production of home-grown timber had increased a hundredfold since the war started.

Exempted until the Ist July. A Substitute Wanted.-

Another head of a business, a Stockton Heath fish and fruit dealer, was exempted until the Ist September, and was advised to get somebody to take his place.

===== 0002601_19160610_art0088.txt | included=true | included =====
Won Four Medals.

On leaving school he entered an examination for the naval service, and came out second in the list, with the result that he was appointed assistant clerk on X.M.S. Victoria, a boat which was rammed while .in • the Mediterranean, most of the crew perishing. Fortunately, Mr. Place was in hospital at Malta at the time. The gentleman who took his place on the ship was drowned. In his career, Mr. Place served on many ships, including the Camperdown, Immortality and Monarch, and was the proud possessor of four medals, one being for his, services in the Persian Gulf, and another for his work during the South African War. He was in two naval actions pre_ vious to the one in which he lost his life.

The photograph we publish was taken when staying in Gibraltar a few years ago. His widow was standing by his side at the time. He himself was an expert photographer, and many of the official photographs of men of war at sea were taken by him. Fleet Paymaster Place leaves a widow and a boy aged 10 years. They are at present at Honiton, Devonshire, the home of her father, Mr. J. E. Rattenbury, who, previous to his re- tirement, held a high post in Sheerness Dock- yard.

On The Defence ; One Of Six

Patriotic Brothers.

Leading Signalman Robert Dixon, who was serving on H.M.S. Defence, comes of a very patriotic family, from Ulverston, near Barrow, for all his brothers—Ave in number—are serving in the Forces. Their sister, Mrs. Hodgson, lives at 6, Florence-street, Latchford. Isador Dixon, who is 27 years of age and married, has been in Canada eight years, and belongs to Princess Patricia's Canadian Light Infantry. • Willie Dixon is a member of the Royal Field Artillery. SERGEANT OF MARINES WOOLLACOTT.

Lost on H.M'.S. Queen Mary.

a few years, by which time he had become a Sergeant. Returning to civil life, he entered the Borough Police Force, and here made 'rapid progress, his capacity. soon gaining for him the confidence of his superiors and comrades in the Force, and especially in the detective depart- ment, where he specialised. For a long time he also acted as Coroner's officer, and when he re- entered the Navy at the outbreak of war his place was taken by Detective-Sergeant Sumner. Mr. Woollacott had been in the Force about twelve years when the war began, and, though not a Reservist, considered it his duty to re- embark on a man of war, and was attached to the Queen Mary in which, it is feared, he eventually perished. Sergeant Woollacott had some interesting and exciting experiences when fulhlling his first term as a Royal Marine, one of which was during his four years spent off the Chinese coast. From his boyhood a keen and able swimmer—he has won several valuable prizes for his prowess—he was bathing when a shark came subtly along and was within an ace of taking him in its deadly grip when he was rescued.

Married Two Sisters.

Sergeant Woollacott married two sisters. His first wife was Miss Mabel Jane Phillips, of Gillingham, Kent, and some time after her de- cease seven years ago, he married her younger sister, Miss Florence Louisa Phillips. It is a interesting fact that his first wife, though always in the best of health until within a few days of her death, not seldom expressed the hone that if she died he would take her sister to the altar.

For several months Mrs. Woollacott and her two sons have been staying at the port where the Queen Mary usually berthed. Their home in Warrington is in Amelia-street. Previously, Sergeant Woollacott resided in Marbury-street. If Sergeant Woollacott is not a survivor, sym- pathy with his family will be of the deepest, for, during the last six months, his eldest brother and father have passed away. His sister, Miss Eva Woollacott, is a teacher at Silver-street School.

RAZORS AND LITERATURE.

===== 0002601_19160610_art0091.txt | included=true | included =====
Needs That Are Keenly Felt By Our

Troops.

The Master Cutler of -Sheffield is collecting spare razors for the use of our troops at the Front. The razors contributed are put in good order and sent forward in lots of 1,000. Already over 160,000 have been collected, and over 135,000 of these have been forwarded to the troops, by whom they are much afpreciated ; the number is, however, still insufficient', and the Master Cutler appeals for further supplies. The condition of the razors does not signify so long as there is a blade which can be reground. Mr. H. Chambers, postmaster, Warrington, will be pleased to forward to the proper quarter any razors which may be delivered to him at the Post Office for the purpose. Books, magazines and literature may also be handed over the counter at the Post Office, and they will be forwarded to the proper quarters. Since last July, by the generosity of the public, the Post Office has maintained an average supply of about 1,000 bags containing books and magazines per week, but the numbers have recently fallen to 800 per week, though our Army has greatly increased. This literature has been greatly appreciated by the recipients, who keeprwriting their -thanks and asking for. more.

THE MIDST OF THE BATTLE.

Our Men Hope To Resume Before Another Two Years.

Writing to a friend in Warrington, Seaman J. Thynne, who was on one of the big Dread- noughts attached to the Grand Fleet, states that the spirit of the crew as they went into action was something to be admired. There was wild cheering when they let rip the first salvo. When the enemy knew they were up against the Grand Fleet they took advantage of the mist and ran for home as fast as steam would allow them. In the midst of battle our men showed the greatest courage and did ,not even seem to think of what might happen if a German shell found its mark in the magazine. Well, the first meeting is over and they were all hoping it would not be two years before they- met them again. Next time they hoped that Grand Admiral " Fog " would keep his nose out of it.

Seaman Thynne, before joining the Navy a few years ago, was employed at Messrs. Pear- son and Knowles as a labourer, and formerly was an "Echo" newsboy. He is a member of the ship's Rugby football team, playing wing threequarter. In a. recent match against a sister ship he scored the winning try, which secured he championship for his sde.

===== 0002601_19160610_art0099.txt | included=true | included =====
POULTRY FOR PROFIT.

KILLI

All birds should be starved for twenty-four hours before killing in order that the crop and intestines may be emptied of food, says "Poultry World." A great amount of loss, arises from neglect of this PrecaUtion. They should be killed by dislocating the neck just where it joins the head, unless the purchaser wishes them to be killed in a special way. Some salesmen like them to be bled by a knife passed through the slot in the roof of the mouth, but this is required in only a few cases. Bleeding is apt to spoil the feathers and soil the packing, and this will reduce the price of the whole con- signment. Dislocation of the neck properly per- formed results in the breaking of the jugular vein, and the blood drains completely from the body veins into the neck.

===== 0002601_19160610_art0100.txt | included=true | included =====
PLUCKING.

Birds should always be plucked while the body is still warm, as the feathers then come out more easily and there is less danger of tearing the skin. Except among the poorer class of buyers a badly- plucked bird is of but little value. Unless pluck- ing is done when the bird is warm, it -should not be done until the bird is quite cold, that is, at least twenty-four hours after killing. In plucking, the operator should hold the bird by the legs, with the head hanging downwards, or. in the case-of turkeys and geese, suspend it by the legs to a cord hung from the roof. Feathers are drawn by a firm rat gentle pull towards the head, this action loosen- ing them from the skin. The plucking should begin at the tail and be continued in the following order: back, neck, wings, sides, legs, and breast. It is unwise to start with the breast, as the surface veins in that part of the body are the last to drain dry, and the carcase will be discoloured if any of these veins are broken. The breast bone should not be broken. Fowls must be plucked clean except for the head and half the neck; turkeys must also be plucked clean, but leaving the feathers on the outer ends of the wings and the tail; in ducks and geese the wings and half the neck must be left unplucked. The legs and feet of all birds should be very clean.

===== 0002601_19160610_art0101.txt | included=true | included =====
FOWL LANGUAGE AT THE FRONT.

A correspondent of the "Tines," who signs him- self "T. H. H." (Lincoln's Inn), gives the follow- ing extract from a letter received from his son who is stationed at a farm "somewhere in France ": " We often hear the sound of a shell coming and yet there is no burst., and we always say, 'Hullo, there's a dud'; but the other day' I was sitting in the yard in the sun when I suddenly heard a shell coming, as I thought, close by. As it appeared to be just about to burst it suddenly stopped. Two minutes later the performance was repeated, and I saw that the noise was being made by a hen. Believe me, this is quite true! Whereupon a cock made a similar noise, and it was the exact imita- tion of a falling shell. I often heard the birds making the _same noise, and I called the attention of the others to it, and they verified my statements. I then questioned the old Frenchman about it, and he says that the birds have learnt to make the noise since the war commeired and keep it up sometimes for a long time."

===== 0002601_19160610_art0102.txt | included=true | included =====
THIS WEEK'S WORK IN BRIEF.

Any empty brooders or coops requiring a coat of Saint or creosote might be taken in hand now. To get everything not in use out of the way should be the poultry-keeper's aim. Brooders and coops put under shelter now are bound to be more ser- viceable next season than are those left out for attention in the autumn.

The January chickens may now be placed on the same rations as the adult stock, but see that they go to roost with a bulging' crop. Do not encourage the early pullets to lay. Feed for bone, and unless they are in a confined run, leave animal -food out of the mash. Examine the nest boxes occasionally for insects and mites. They are favourite breeding grounds for these vermin.

Clean and fresh-looking eggs are always more acceptable to the buyer than are eggs that are dirty and of doubtful appearance. No egg can be made to look exactly the same as a clean new laid. Washing removes the bloom. Clean nests does away with the necessity for washing. Stained eggs should be kept for the household use. As should all undersized or abnor- mal eggs.

Your eggs are your advertisement. See that they form a good one. Do not hold over eggs for an improvement in prices. Let them go to market at least once a week.—" The Poultry World."

===== 0002601_19160610_art0103.txt | included=true | included =====
LITTLE tHINGS THAT COUNT.

Feeding Growing Chickens.—When chickens get from three to four months old they should not be stinted of food, but allowed as much as they will oat. .They have to make bone and frame, and to do this a plentiful supply of food is needed. Any- thing in the form of an oat diet is good. At this stage a little green bone will prove beneficial, and will tend to keep the chickens well up on their legs. Allow them as much range as possible, or have a series of small runs, and change them round every Week or two. Don't forget to separate the sexes, for then the youngsters will do much better, be- sides preventing squabbling and fighting. Should the cockerels show any inclination to the latter proceeding, run an old cock with them, and his presence will bring peace into the flock.

Care of Combs.—The combs of many breeds re- quire care in other respects than avoiding excess of animal food. Heat is very injurious. In all cases where combs are wanted small and neat the chicks shquld be brought up in cool and airy roost- ing placeeas soon as taken from the mother, which should be before the combs get any height. If this is not done, the mere pressure of the hen's body tends, during the youngest and most euscep- tible stage, to bend over or twist the comb. This applies even more to single upright combs, which must be kept straight, as in Leghorn and Minorca cockerels. Later, when the combs are growing fast, if there is reason to fear disaster, it is often of the greatest service to sponge the comb gently every night with hazeline tincture, or to wile= it with hazeline cream. This is not only a Mild though effective astringent, but has a specific action in re- pelling congestion of blood to the part where applied, and will. help much to keep a comb firm and within bounds.

Broody Hens.—lf you do not wish to set the hens now getting broody, do not put off "breaking up" their maternal inclination. The longer it con- tinues the poorer their condition, and the longer you will have to wait for their return to laying. A coop with slatted bottom hung up from the floor makes the best place for confining the hens. Weeding out the Chickensh—Cull now. Every de- fective chicken kept only adds to the expense, con- tributes to the crowding of better birds, and lowers the standard of the flock. The closer you cull the quicker you will get to the top. Clearing off the Old Hens.—lt will soon be time to clear off the two-year-Old hens, which should be done as soon as they show signs of giving up lay- ing. Feed them liberalfy tø put on flesh, and thus a more remunerative price will be obtained for them. •

Keep the Drinking Vessels Clean.—Earthenware drinking vessels quickly get slimy and dirty. Cleanse them regularly, and tow and again scour and scald them with boiling water. The good health of the birds will amply repay. for the time given to the work. Keep the vessels in a shady corner.

RURAL TRIBUNAL.

Woolston Claimant Who Wants To Return To Russia.

RECAPTURED COPPER WORKS.

The Warrington Rural Tribunal resumed its sjttings on Thursday, when a number of im- portant and interesting appeals were heard. A remarkable case was that of a temporary resident of Woolston claiming exemption. His home and business is in the Caucasus, the scene of the recent hard fighting. He had to flee with his wife when the Turks invaded Russian terri- tory, and came over to England, but with the reoccupation of the territory by our Allies he hopes to return to the scene of his former labours.

===== 0002601_19160610_art0108.txt | included=true | included =====
DRIVEN OUT BY TURKS.

Applicant Whose Home And Work Is

In The Caucasus.

A married man, 36 years, of Woolston, claimed though he was a British subject, that he had no home in England (his home being situated in the Caucasus Tiflis district on the Caucasus Copper Company's premiges) from which they had been driven out by the Turks. He hail an agreement with the company to resume opera- tions for the production of copper for the Russian Government, and hoped to return there as soon as possible. Correspondence was read from the Caucasus Copper Company dated March 13th, 1915, to the effect that they had received a cablegram from Batoum informing them that the Russian mili- tary forces had occupied Dzansoul, and that no serious damage had been done to the property. The company intended to resume operations as soqn as possible. Another letter, dated May 25th, 1916, stated that the Russian authorities were anxious to increase the output of copper in their country. Negotiations were now pro- co

_Jeding for the purpose of securing an advance from the Russian Government to enable them to recommence operations. They expected to hear shortly that all formalities were completed, and they desired to know whether the applicant could obtain permission to return to the position whicfi he occupied in the company's employment before the Turks took possession of the property. The company were reluctant to ask exemption for anyone liable for service, but they felt that the fact that the work to be performed would be such an enormous advantage in enabling them to produce copper as quickly as possible. They asked whether he could leave for Russia at short notice directly information came that the finan- cial negotiations were completed. .

_ Appellant's wife appeared to support the application, and stated that her husband was at present ill in bed; but they were exected to go when he was better. Her husband had lived in the Caucasus five years, and she had been there two years.

A Member of the Tribunal: Why did you come back? The Wife: We were driven out by the Turks. The premises are situated on the Russian and Turkish frontier.

The Chairman: You are anxious to go ba,!k- to drive the Turks out?

The Wife! I hope so. in the world.

They took all we owned

Mr. Furness: And they got the copper mines? The Wife: Yes, but they have been driven out. and the company are now in possession. - The Rev. A. M. Mitchell: Do you like that part of the world? The Wife: Very much. The Tribunal granted one month's exemption. but if the husband was fit to go out before that he must communicate with the Clerk of the Tribunal.

===== 0002601_19160610_art0109.txt | included=true | included =====
Publican's Appeal Refused.

The licensee of a hotel in Fearnhead, appealed on the ground of domestic hardship. He state.' that in his mare time, when the hotel was not open, he assisted his brother, who was engaged in making wire goods for several firms employed exclusively on munitions. In the event of him joining the Army he would have to give up his business, as his wife was unable to manage it in his absence. They had two children. Giving up business would mean a big financial loss to him on fixtures and other business appliances. Mr. W. Furness pointed out that brewery com- panies were granting ,authority to the wives of licensees who were called up. The Rev. A. M. Mitchell: And they manage better than the men sometimes.

The appeal was dismissed. Waiting For Naval Commission.

Since he lodged an appeal for exemption, a licensee at Penketh stated, he had decided to join the Navy. He was in communication with the Admiralty with regard to a commission in a particular section of the Navy. He withdraw his application for conditional exemption in favour of a temporary one until such time as the Admiralty had completed negotiations. Exemption until August 16th was granted. Need for War Hospital Staffs.

Eight men, including two assistant clerks, assistant storekeeper, electric wireman, cartar, stoker, and two masseurs, employed at the Lord Derby War Hospital. applied for conditional exemption. The Clerk pointed out that all the men were engaged on the staff. A Member of the Tribunal: We have heard they are tumbling over each other at the hos- pital. Mr. Furness (the military representative) said the men were considered to be indispensable to the working of the hospital. It was a mistake Ito say they are tumbling over each other. It was necessary that they should have a large staff ready at any moment to cope with an influx of patients. In the Whitecross Hospital they could do without 90 per cent, of the staff, but they were bound to keep them in expectation of an influx of wounded men, and the same con- ditions operated at the Lord Derby War llos- pital. The Rev. A. vl. Mitchell: It is not a question of being overstaffed, but if some of the men could not be dispensed with and women sub- stituted.

Five conditional exemptions were granted, and the applications of the stoker and the two masseurs adjourned for one month. Tannery Office Manager Exempted.

Mr. Cyril Gough, on behalf of Messrs. Wm. Mortimer and Co., Ltd., applied for exemption for their office manager who had full control of all the confidential and financial matters which passed through the office. They had no other inside raan.—The Military Representative offered n objection. He pointed out that the Orford Tannery were models of what a tannery out to do in the matter of recruiting.—Condi- tional exemption was granted. Horse Dealer and Forage Merchant.

Mr. T. S. Steel, on behalf of a horse dealer and forage merchant in business on his own account at Orford, applied for conditional exemption. He stated that he had been in business for two years. He supplied horses for the Government, but at present they only required a limited number. He also supplied horses for big con- tractors and carters in the district, used for commercial purposes. There was no one to manage the business, and his departure would ,mean financial ruin. He had a considerable amount of money invested in hay, which he had bought to sell again, and in addition large sums had been spent in purchasing horses. He also had long leases on his house and stebles, and had spent money in improving his stabling accommodation.

In reply to the Chairman (Mr. G. Mather), he said the reason he had not attested was because he did not think it was safe in view of hie business obligations. He understood attests- lion meant enlistment, offering your services for the country with the intention of leaving busi- ness.

Mr. Farneas : Your interests then ire much more important than the interests of yEur coun- try? • Applicant: No. I don't say that!

Mr. Furness : Your appeal is on financial grounds primarily, but seeing that you do not carry a vellY large stock, I cannot see how you can be affected financially. The Government will make an allowance up to £2 weekly. Mr. Steel: He makes considerably more than that out of his business, and it would be a hard- ship if he' had to go. He claims that he came under the head of the "one-man business," upon which his wife depends. Exemption until August 16 was granted. Appellant: I don't think that is of any use to me. .

Tlte Chairman:, Would you sooner go now? Because we can alter our decision.

THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, JUNE 10, 1916.

LOCAL FACTS AND FANCIES.

By CRITIC. "Won't you buy my pretty flbwers?" was a cry heard from every quarter last week-end.

Nor did it pass by unheeded. By dinner-time everyone seemed to be sporting the familiar Alexandra rose.

One lady whom I carne across told me that I might as well be dead as out of the fashion.

It was rumoured that the event was to be postponed for a fortnight owing to the Lord Roberts's flag day being held the week previous.

The promoters evidently gathered pluck at the eleventh hour, and the affair ended in a bril- liant success.

The news of Lord Kitchener's death fell like a bombshell on the patriotic little village.

Shortly after the news arrived, the hospital flag was noticed to be at half mast.

I was sorry to notice that very few residents followed the hospital example.

Not a single flag could be seen throughout the whole village.

The Sunday matinee at the Star Kinema in aid of the blind heroes' fund seems to have created considerable interest in the village.

If the weather clerk will only smile next Monday, things ought to be merry and bright at Statham.

The phrase most heard in this quarter at the present time is "What if it rains?" I wonder.

Corporal Henry Brookes, our St. George hero, returned to the Front last Wednesday.

Captain Wilde, who is at present enjoying a short furlough, looks none the worse for his soldiering experiences.

His work chiefly consists of keeping the cap- tured rat in its cage.

He is guarding German prisoners at the Isle of Man camp..

While going along the station platform the other day I .caught a fleeting glance of Lance- Corporal R. Dixon, of the R.A.M.C., another cf our brave lads enjoying a well-earned rest.

Sergeant T. W. Field (our local D.C.M.), who was so seriously ill some time ago, has returned to Heaton Park for light duty. He has not yet received his medal.

The local Tribunal is now getting really busy.

Captain J. D. Thompson, the well-known Junior Unionist orator, has been promoted Adjutant of his battalion.

I hear that Oughtrington is going to follow the Lymm example and hold a cafe chantant during the coming week.

The success of a thing makes it quite an epidemic. "It's catching."

Several married men in the, district have this week answered the calling up of their groups.

"Business as usual" is our mottii for next Monday.

Even the Post Office have decided not to inconvenience .the output of shells.

Plumber's Successful Appeal.

A plumber (33) appealed for total exemption on the groands that he would suffer serious financial hardships if called to the Colours, also that he was a partner in a firm Of painters, decorators and plumbers, and that it is in the national interests that some experienced plumber should be left in the district.—Appli- cant was represented by Mr. G. L. Welford, solicitor, of Manchester, who said that his'client was a salaried partner. During the last two years the Other partner had had to give up doing active work owing to illness, and was only assist- ing in the management at present. During - these two years the bulk of the work had fallen on the applicant. When war broke out there were four plumbers employed in the plumbing branch, two single men and two married men, but the single men very soon joined the Colours, and left them with but one other man besides applicant. This man was over military age, and was constantly employed on special work, which left the other work to be done solely by appli- cant. Applicant also did all the clerical work

connected- with the firm. In normal times the firm generally employed about twelve men, but at the present they had to be satisfied with a staff of six men, and they had work in hand at the present time that would take them until October to complete. Applicant was a married man with three children.—By the Court: The firm had not tried to get clerical assistance. Applicant had done the clerical work for seven or eight years. The firm -had tried to gct another plumber, but with no success. The business might stand a lady clerk part time; there was not enough to do for full time. It was further stated that if applicant had to it would mean the closing down of the business. The Court decided to grant temporary con- ditional exemption until September Ist, subject to no application being made for any other employe.—Mr. Welford : Could we not have until October?—The Chairman: The Tribunal have considered the case very carefully. Better Fustian Cutting Than Milking.

A farm bailiff who appealed for a man (36), stated that there was great difficulty at the present time in obtaining suitable labour. Have you ever tried women labour F—Yes, I have had two women working for me. How did you manage them gave them tea and bread and butter, but they Were not satis- fied with that, and asked for their money. I gave it them. Could they milk?—They were better fustian cutting than milking. How long has applicant worked for youP—Two years.

He further stated that if the work was left entirely to him he would not attempt to bring a mowing machine out,

The Court decided to grant conditional exemp- tion until October Ist.

Farmer Secures Exemption.

A farmer applied for absolute exemption on the grounds that he was the occupier of a farm of 96 acres, 50 of which were under the plough. He had two men and two boys, and had had women for odd days. He had no tine who could work the farm for him. He intended to appeal further, for he could not do without one man. It was a busy time at present.—The Court decided to grant conditional exemption, subject to his remaining at the present work. Hampermaker Appeals For Man.

Most young men in the trade had joined the Colours, and he usually had a staff of about ten men. Their work was chiefly making hampers for agricultural and market gardening purposes. How many men have you at present ?---Only the only appealed for and a son. What about the son?—He hits had ill-health for some time, and.could not be relied upon. How long has the man been with you ?—Over four years.

after leaving school joined the local company of Volunteers as a boy bugler. Since that time his mind 'was given to the Army. When be reached the age of eighteen he decided to take up soldiering as a profession, and joined the Regular Army as a member of the Cheshire Regiment. His military abilities were speedily recognised, and promotion came Mt. For some time•past he has held the rank of a Sergeant of the line. Being a member of the Regular Army, he was one of the first to be on the battlefield of Flanders, and went through the remarkable retreat from Mons, and through all the opening stages of the war until the battle of the Aisne. Here he had the. misfortune to be hit with an incendiary bomb, dTopped from hokile aircraft, and was sent over severely wounded to Chester. During the whole of the journey he was un- conscious. After' sufficiently recovering lo enable him to leave hospital, he had the mis- fortune to be severely burned owing to a box of matches igniting in his pocket, and while undergoing treatment for these injuries he con- tracted pneumonia, from which he has just died.

Deceased was 25 years of age, and prior to joining the Army seven years ago. was employed as an errand boy for F. E. Evans, chemist, The Cross. His brother, Corporal Richard Lewis, Royal Field Horse Artillery, is at present somewhere in France.

It is expected that the funeral will take place at the Parish Church at the beginning of the week.

A WORD ABOUT THE CAMERON HIGHLANDERS.

By PRIVATE ERNEST SHAW. [Continued.]

MARCH, 1915.

March! What a month ! Cold and rain, with the usual March winds added. That was the lot that befel "Tommy "—shall I say the great "Tommy "?—during the time that he occupied the trenches of France and Flanders. For " Tommy " is truly "great." Even when re- markably heavy odds were against him, and his best pals were going under by his side, he was always thew, stuck at his post, and nothing but death could shift him. When the critical time was over, and the Huns had been driven back, " Tommy " would come marching in with a smiling face, whistling some popular ditty, as if he had been to witness some first-class foot- ball match. That's the sort of " Tommy " the British woman rears. That's the man that the entire world envies; that's the man who has put fear into the hearts of the entire German race. But this is getting off my tale. As my mind wanders back to the days of March, 1915, I can- not help but shudder at the reflection of some of my -experiences. March 2.—Relieved by Scots Guards, after 48 hours in firing line and supports. Went into billets at

4th.—Returned to breastworks, relieving the Scots Guards and London Scottish.

6th.—Returned to billets we occupied on the 2nd.

Bth.—lteturned again to breastworks. Pte. Burt awarded the

9th.—Pte. Mackenzie and Sergt. Cummings awarded the D.C.M.

10th.—Held the Huns at bay by intermittent rifle fire dtiring the fight at llth.—Lieut. Steward joined from United Kingdom. Relieved Scots Guards and London Scottish in evening. 13th.—The Rev. J. Whitfield, C.F. (R.C.) be- came attached to the battalion. ' A draft of 158 arrived from Scotland via , conducted by two officers.

14th.—Relieved Scots Guards and London Scottish in breastworks. 16th.—Scots Guards and London Scottish re- lieved us in evening..

18th.—Relieved Scots Guards and London Scot- tish again in breastworks. 20th!—Lieut. Gordon Cumming and two men sent down for instruction in machine gunnery. 22nd.—Loyal North Lancashires took over from us at . Marched back to miles). .

MAY DAY.

WORKED, FOUGHT, WOUNDED, WORKING.

St. Helens Cable Company's Employes Box The

Patriotic Compass.

Monday's Great Festival For Children And Old People.

Elaborate preparations are now in progress towards the completion of all arrangements for Monday's great festival, and Statham itself is one buzz of excitement. The programme is now completed. The pro- ceedings will open with a procession, headed by the Grappenhall Subscription Band, which starts from Cross Brow at one o'clock, going via Booth's Hill to the village, round the Cross, returning to the field via Lymm Station. On the field the public are to be treated to May- pole and garland dances, executed by the chil- dren, sports, and various amusements. Tea will be served in the Statham Schools (by kind consent of the Rector and churchwardens) about 4 p.m. Afterwards prizes are to be distributed to all school children, and old people over the age of sixty. The band will play for public dancing until dusk. During the past week Councillor Frederick Lockyer, the organiser, informed an " Examiner " man that although they had suffered severe criticisms from too patriotic subscribers, they were pleased. to be able to show a balance in hand from.last year's festival of £lO 4s. 24d. What can be wrong with the festival we fall to see. The parties that benefit are the children and old people. Their fathers, brothers and their sons are away nobly doing their duty, and it is for us wha are able to see that they get a little respite from the trials of war. It is gratifying to know that after last year's festival several letters were received from the men in the trenches sending their approval of the continuance of the children's pleasure.

Private James Jennings. of the Man- chester Regiment, says: "I should have for- gotten all about Easter had it not been for the nice services we had here.
