Firm's Splendid Record. "Out of a total of 120 men who have been released for service with H.M. Forces," we are informed by a St. Helens Cable Co. official, "up to the time of having the photograph taken these are the only three who have been returned. All the others are still on active service, with the exception of four men only, who have been killed. As a firm we feel very grateful that the casualty list has been so small, and we are proud of our record of having releaseil for mili- tary service fully one-half of our employes of military age. We keep in constant touch with each one of our men, and regularly send them parcels of comforts, which are subscribed ff r by the members of the staff and employes of ;he firm. We almost daily recerre letters of appreciation from our soldiers, which we are keeping by us as a record of these dark days. They will be preserved in the annals of the com- pany to hand down to future generations:, -

will always be so. I can tell you there is not much pleasure here. It is so hot, and during the day we are nearly worried with flies. We are always glad when the sun, goes down, and the flies settle for the night, for then we are able to get a little amusement. I have not seen the pyramids yet, but I am hoping to do so before we leave 'here. At present we are miles away from them." Private James. Jennings is a well- known local lad, and is looked upon hy "Ex- aminer" readers as a gifted correspondent.

Heart Fai I ure.—We regret to announce the sudden death of Miss Maud Mort (41), which took place at her residence, Booth's Hill, on Friday week. Miss Mort became ill on Thurs- day night, but seemed to have recovered on the Friday morning, and was quite bright and chatty. About 113.45 a.m. she was noticed to collapse on the couch. She died before any assistance could be rendered. At the inquest, conducted by Deputy Coroner White at the Police Station on Monday afternoon, Dr. Burrows said that death was due to heart failure, caused by sudden exertion after vomit- ing. The jury returned a verdict to that effect. The funeral took place at the Parish Church on Tuesday afternoon, conducted by the Rev. G. B. Thurston. The principal mourners were Mother, Florrie and Eddie (sisters), Mrs. Brown (cousin), and Mrs. J. Cadman. Floral tributes were sent by: "Father, Maher and Eddie," "Jack and Florrie," "Kenneth," "Aunt Mary and family," Mr. and Mrs. J. Cadman and family, Mr. and Mrs. Tonge and family. The funeral arrangements were conducted by Mi. W. G. Groves, Booth's Hill.

Mouth Organs Provide Pleasure for Soldiers. —Mr. J. T. Milne, Leahurst, Brook-road, has received the following letter from Pte. Jack Cheetham, Cheshire Regiment, who is at pres- ent serving with the Indian Expeditionary Forces. It was sent from a British General Hospital, where the gallant soldier has been recuperating from sickness: "When you see the above address you will naturally think that I am ill in hospital, but such is not the case; it is quite the opposite, as I commenced duty on Easter Sunday in the office. I was pleased to receive the pamphlet you sent me, notifying me about the organ recitals that have been taking place at the parish church in aid of the mouth organ fund. They must have provided a few comfortable hours for some of our fellows. 'I can assure you that the cause is one of the best, and the instruments are the means of many a pleasant sing-song, when nothing else is avail- able. Give my kind regards to all the mem- bers of the Sunday school class." Pte. Jack Cheetham. is a well-known local lad, who enlisted soon after the outbreak of war. He was eventually drafted out • to the Dar- danelles, where he remained until the great evacuation. .In civil life he was a grocer's assistant employed by the late Frank Warbur- ton, The Cross. For several years he was a chorister at the Parish Church.

Alexander Rose Day Result • Nearly M.-- Thanks to the energetic President, Mr. T. F. W. Dixon, and his assistant, Mr. G. A. Cooke, the Alexandra Rose Day was held as usual last Saturday, and ended a brilliant success. About 3,500 roses were sold, at anything from one penny upwards, and the magnificent sum of £lB 146. was raised, and has been duly forwarded to the proper quarter. Calling at the distributing centre last Saturday night, our representative found it quite a hive of industry, and he was informed that although a large number of ladies who usually sold roses on Alexandra Day were occupied doing war work, there had been no difficulty in getting the necessary help, and the scheme had run quite pleasantly. About 8 p.m. all the unsold roses from the outlying districts were gathered in, and the President and his assistant, with grim determination themselves canvassed the people in the village, and by nine o'clock every rose was sold. During the week a letter of thanks from the Executive Committee has been received thanking the President, and expressing appreciation of the magnificent re- sults.

The following ladies took out baskets from the distributing centre :—Mrs. Slaney, Miss Slaney, Miss R. Oddie, Miss Barker, Misses H. and F. Dixon, Miss Pearse, Miss F. Holt, Miss Thomason, Miss Priestner, Miss Telfer, Miss Ingham, Miss Davies, • Miss Moffatt, Miss Gleave, and Miss Carr.

STOCKTON HEATH.

APPLETON MARRY AS TIP,

Suggestion Put Forward At Parochial

Committee.

At the commencement of the meeting of the Stockton Heath Parochial Committee, held in the Council Schools on Tuesday evening, Mr. G. Pilling thanked the members for again electing him to the chair, and said he would do his utmost faithfully to carry out his duties. A letter was read from .a contractor, Mr Spencley, saying that he had received notice to discontinue using the tip in Fairfield-road, but had been fortunate in securing one at Walton.

Mr:H. J. Westbrook thereupon proposed that the Clerk communicate with Mr. Beresford Melville asking him if he would agree to the Appleton Quarry being used as a tip, the Coun- cil to give an assurance that only ordinary refuse would be tipped. Mr. Westbrook also thought that Lower Walton might be asked to join in, and the tip would last them for years.

The proposition was unanimously agreedto. There were presont at the meeting: Messrs. George Pilling (chairman), J. 11. Clarke, F. Warburton, F. Pearson, H. J. Westbrook, F. Rose, A. Bottomley, W. Hallows, J. Hallows, E. Bradshaw (clerk to the Parish Council), and G. W. Lloyd, A. S. Wright and W. E. Hough, officials to the committee.

PENKETH.

In the Great Sea Fight —lt is with great pleasure we see two of our brave sailor lads safe at home after the turmoil of the past week. Albert, second son of Mr. R. Jones, butcher, Warrington-road, Penketh, has been in the thick of the fighting. As a stoker he had an anxious time, but all's well that ends well. Two of his brothers answered to the call for King and country. The eldest, Robert, a lance- corporal in the Fifth 'Dragoon Guards, was wounded in France in the hand and arm, in- capacitating him from further active service. Sam, the third son, is another lance-corporal, undergoing training for the great event. The other sailor is George Carrington, A. 8., who has been away for two years and a half. Part of the time he has been operating along the Mediterranean and Dardanelles. The last time he returned home to his father's house in the Park, Penketh, he had barely' time to show him- self when he was called away 'again. Two of.

GREAT SANKEY.

Wedding.—Last Saturday, at St. Mary's Church, Great Sankey, a marriage wag solemnised between Miss Dorothy Clarke, youngest daughter of Mr. George Clarke, head, master of Sankey Council' School, and Mrs - Clarke, and Mr. Harvey Turner, Church FarM, Plemstall, Cheshire. The Rev. J. R. Jones offi- ciated. The bride, who was given away by her father, looked charling in a cream costume* and silk hat to match. Miss Laura Turner (sister of the bridegroom), who made a very pretty bridesmaid, was dressed in. a Saxe-blue costume, and Leghorn hat, trimmed with blue, to- match, and Mr. Fred Turner 'acted as best man for his brother. Miss Standish presided at the organ, "The Voice that breathed o'er Eden" and "Lead us, Heavenly Father," being the hymns chosen, and Mendelssohn's Wedding March. A large number of valuable presents were received, amongst them two 'which will be specially prized by the young couple, one from the Sunday school children, a silver flower stand, and another from the scholars of the weekday school, a case of fish knives and zorks• At both schools Miss Clarke had been a teacher for some considerable time. Owing to the bride- groom having to join his group on Monday the - wedding was very quiet.

BURTON WOOD.

ENCLOSING THE TIP.

Mitigating A Nuisance Greatly Complained Of.

At the monthly meeting of the Warrington Rural District Council on Wednesday, the Medical Officer of Health (Dr. Murray) reported that he had met at the refuse tip at Collins Green the Chairman and other representatives of the Newton Urban District Council, and observed a very decided improvement in the way in which loose paper, &c., had been collected. The representatives of the Newton Council promised that they would adopt further measures, such as wire netting, to prevent paper blowing over the road and fields and also, as far as they could, keep people away from the tip. They proposed to have a watchman there when the other men had left at night, and on Satur- days and Sundays. The Rev. A. M. Mitchell: I don't think that putting up of wire netting will effect what w‘ want if there is a strong wind.

Dr. Murray: The watchman will gather up the paper and burn it. They are also going to ask the inhabitants of Newton not to throw much Paper into the ashpits.

===== 0002601_19160610_art0126.txt | included=true | included =====
"WIGS ON THE GREEN."

Warrington & District Farmers Spurn Irish Labourers. Judged by a discussion of the Warrington and Distiict War Agricultural Committee, which met in the Griffin Hotel on Wednesday evening, local farmers will have nothing to do with Irish labourers, despite the fact that the shortage Of farm workers is giving them grave concern. The Chairman (Mr. J. Ball) initiated the debate, first pointing out the necessity for more labour on the farms and wondering whether, ' they should suggest to the Board of Agrirmltuto any means for persuading Irishmen to return • from their own country to English land. He condemned the unpatriotic action or the Irish in running away when fearing eonscriptinu, and only last Sunday had expressed his dis- pleasure by sending away from his gate " thrte strapping young fellows," although he wanted labour badly. However, the question was •whether they should give priority to their feel- ings towards these men or have them because of the urgent necessity of producing food? Several members took part in the discussion, one saying he would rather his stuff should rot in the land than that Irishmen should handle it; another, that if Irishmen came he was sure English labourers would put on their jackets as • a protest, or even fight them, and that °nee the Irish had been promised non-interference by the Government tbey would no sooner be on the English farms than they would go into the munition works for higher money and their use- fulness in the production of food would be over. There were about a dozen members present, and a motion that the Government be asked to persuade Irishmen to return to England found - no seconder, and the discussion ended in con- demnatory sentiments regarding the disloyalty of those who had sailed across the Channel on the advent of conscription.

Naming the Twins.

You have heard of the twins, Kate and Duplicate? earnestly inquires the "Kansas City Times." So had an Arkansas mother of twins, who liked the idea and wanted to adopt it. Her name being Kate, it was easy to name the girl twin in her honour, but Duplicate seemed hardly to fit the boy. This proved a stumbling block for some time, but after considerable dis- cussion it was surmounted. In delicate compli- ment to the boy's father, who was a moonshiner, it was decided to call him Lubricate. This story is just made up, of course, but it ii as true as most Arkansas stories.

A PICIURISED ROMANTIC NOVEL BY ROY lIMCCARDELL ADAPTED FROM THE SENSATIONAL -FLYING A-, SERIAL FILM; BY . ALE C J. BRA II.:

IThe characters in this story are purely imaginary, living

SYNOPSIS OF. PRECEDING CHAPTER.

A bitter feud exists between Colonel Arthur Stanleigh and his cousin, Judge Lamar Stanleigh, engendered over a family heirloom, The Diamond from the Sky, found in a meteor by an adventurer ancestor. The feud is further augmented by the fact ,that the Stanleigh Earldom in Warwickshire Inas, come to a son of the Virginian families of the Stanleighs. When a. daughter is born to the wife of Colonel Stanleigh, the disappointed father buys a .male gipsy child and substitutes him as heir. 131scovering the sale of her baby, Hagar, the gipsy rctother, drags herself to Stanleigh Hall, hoping to leqain him. She arrives just as her baby's birth is °eine registered, and Colonel Stanleigh, seeing her, falls unconscious across the library table.

CHAPTER 11. AN EYE FOR AN EYE.

Long ago Dr. Lee warned his friend against iintlue excitement; but the sight which met his eyes w. aB too unexpected .to be warded against. Chuck- ling inwardly at the defeat of his rival, he raised a,is eyes in time to see Matt Harding dragging 1-agar from the door of his room, his rough, great and stifling her cry. The mother had come for tjle child he had so callously sold a few hours ?afore. There came a vision before Colonel Stan- 'Sigh of all his plans miscarrying. At the last moment the little Blair might step in and succeed to all the honours of the Stanleigh family. But the danger was over almost before it was realised.

For nearly four years the old negro nurse watched and guarded the baby, girl with all the wealth of love that a great mother-heart could bestow. At length Colonel Stanleigh realised that it was becoming dangerous to keep the child in the house, and wrote to Dr. Lee asking him to call the following day at midnight, take the child away, and adopt her as his own. Other plans were in the flaking at the time. Hagar had returned to Fair- fax, and hungered for a sight of her boy. That afternoon from the cover of a wood she watched the boY and the man who had robbed her; the man the countryside regarded as the father of little Arthur. ,Raga r had come back to Fairfax to carry away the !oily, and the sight she witnessed only intensified iler determination. That very night she would force a way into the house a,nd steal quietly away. It was almost midnight,: Colonel Stanleigh sat alone in the library idly fingering The Diamond from the Sky while he read the superscription on the cryvelope beside him. As the years had crept over ,13-fif. and Arthur was already showing signs of a headstrong child, he decided.,to safeguard thC interests of his little daughter. If the boy did not deserve the honours in store for him, revelation of the truth would, deprive him of the Stanleigh titles and estates. The document should be handed to Dr. Lee. He would know how to make use of the secret were it necessary. "I have come for my child," said a voice behind

Turning, the terrified Colonel gazed into the reso- 'lite face of Hagar. With a cry he staggered to his feet. and, twist- -1/,g round, fell back dead. " A judgment!" breathed Hagar, as she swert 74i3znond and document from the table, and went 'a search of her boy. Lying soundly asleep in the our-post bed was her son—her own sou, putative heir of Stanleigh Hall. She marked the elegance of his surroundings as the full moon shone in the 'window. What should she do? If she left her child there she would be cheating the cheaters. What would be the wild upbringing she could give her son in comparison with all that would be his as heir of Stanleigh Hall? " Pll leave you here, my boy," she whispered. Remain as the heir of Stanleigh Hall, and I will iCeeD The Diamond from the Sky for you." Then Hagar heard footsteps. Gazing out crtiously, she saw the old nurse coming down- firs With with the little girl, the rightful heir of Stan- Hall. Hagar listened intently. Lying on the ii,brarY floor was the dead master of the house, and tat knew that in a minute the whole place would nel izt .r an uproar. A wild shriek arose from the .ed nurse as she entered the library and saw her master, the man who had been so kind to her N, her husband, stretched lifeless on 11 e Leaving little Esther in the room crying " Wake up, daddie, and kiss me," the nurse ran for her husband. Hearing the arrival of Dr. Lee's gig, the old people went to the door and broke the dreadful news to him. This gave Hagar an oppor- tunity to satisfy her longing for revenge- Picking the child, she said to the dead man lying at b.f. feet, "An eye for an eye. You took my child; I Will take yours," and fled behind the curtain of the bight.

Eighteen times the full seasons ran their course before the veil, was lifted upon the Stanleigh family 'drama. Arthur Stanleigh, master of Stanleigh Hall, *as wasting his time in riotous living, and with ttlair, his cousin, Was well known in all fast circles '3l Virginian society. Dr. Henry Lee, pressed with tile weight of years, was guardian of the young Zan. and wondered what the end of it would be. 4kte old coloured nurse and Ned had passed none, remained save the doctor, and Hagar,-the ‘7DBY woman, whom he had sought far and wide for rears. Fate, the weaver of destinies, in the 11,11aDe of Hagar, at last came upon the scene. If 'lle doctor had wondered whether, in his bait! of °,22e dead man and his love for anniicr, l bad not gone wrong; Hagar, queen of the gipsies. wondered. . if her vengeance had not gone all awry. Esther titanleigh—or, as she was known, Esther Harding— /Pas a beautiful and sweetly dispositioned young Wornan. The wild mother-love Hagar bore for the !c:/1 sold from her had passed to sweet Esther. So Was that Hagar returned to Virginia, resolved ain to sacrifice her very heart. facet of The Diamond from the Sky. Now he would like to see her boy in his manhood, and the wild hope possessed her that he might fall in love with Esther. Long had this been a cherished desire, and Hagar had come to Fairfax to ask Dr. Lee to take over the trust his dead friend wi 41. ed to lay upon him. Calling one of her tribe she sent him to Dr. Lee with a note: " I love Esther, but feel that she should have her chance in life. Will you adopt her? Answer by messenger.—Hagar Harding." The terse note, full of possibilities, had come, Dr. Lee thought, in answer to his prayers. Here was an opportunity to make restitution; but first Hagar owed him an explanation. To the gipsy camp went the old man, demanding to know what the woman meant. " Where have you kept the child all these years? I have -hunted high and low for you, and now you come to me, appealing to give Esther a chance in life. Does it mean that at last you realise the wrong you have done her? See of what you have deprived her." Dr. Lee was angry, but his rage withered before the 'tense indignation of the woman. " How dare you speak to me-like this?" she cried. "You, the very doctor who was partner in the crime whi‘a robbed me of my boy. The stain of that crime should have blackened your soul, but "—in mood—" I have not come to revile you. I 1: ve Esther too dearly to wreck her future. Take her, adopt her, and give her a chance in life. Do not let her remain a gipsy girl." "I will accept your trust upon one condition."

"And that?" asked Hagar. " That you give me The Diamond from the Sky to hand to Rather when she marries or I die."

" But why? My son is entitled to it, is he not, as master of Stanleigh Hall?" "As master of Stanleigh Hall he may be truth be hid, but he is not worthy of it. If I to take Esther the diamond must come with her."

For the sake of the sweet girl she loved so deafly Hagar consented, and the doctor took Esther back with him. Within an hour the camp was struck. Heartbroken and weary, Hagar and her tribe kit the place that held so many tragic memories for the woman.

At their parting there had been one gift of Hagar's that the girl gladly took with her, a pair of homing pigeons. "Take these, my dear daugh- ter. If ever you are in trouble and need me, send a message by the birds. Their homing-place is our gipsy rendezvous in the Blue Ridge. Even if I am not there when the message comes, some of our tribe will be. They will know where I am and bring me word." Although the proud women of the countryside looked askance, within a short time Esther was belle of the xlistrict, and the ivyclad Porch of the doctor's old house saw a nightly gathering of all the gay spirits of Fairfax. There came a night in June, and in the moonlight were four young men all paying court to the happy Esther. Chief among them were the Stanleigh cousins, Stanleigh and Blair. Sorne slight attention to his cousin roused Arthur to a tempest of jealousy. PrettilY rebuked by the girl, he left in a huff. Then Esther teased the triumphant Blair, and he, too, became angry and departed. Sulkily straying by the house, he saw the light gleaming from the doctor's study. Curious, he peered through the window and started hack, clenching his hands. What jewel was this in the hands of the old man? Could it be the Stanleigh diamond, the Stanleigh "charm against harm," of which his mother had so often spoken? Since the death of Colonel Stanleigh the Stanleigh heirloom had been missing, and his mother had often whispered her suspicions to her son. "Dr. Lee was alone with Colonel Stanleigh when he died. Who else but he has taken and hidden away The Diamond from the Sky?"

The obsession of the -desire for this priceless heir- loom had been born and bred in Blair Stanleigh. Here was an opportunity to gain the jewel, and he determined there and then tp return and secure the diamond, come what might. With the mad desire in his mind he went home and prepared for the deed.

In the library at Stanleigh Hall the young heir mused and dreamt of Esther. His guitar lay near him, and he picked it up and gently strummed it. In his mind's eye he saw himself serenading sweet Esther and begging her gentle pardon in the moon- light, should she come to her window to listen. The romantic idea suited his mood, and he took the guitar and hastened again to the doctor's house.

- Blair Stanleigh has -clambered through the study window, closed it, and drawn the blind. He has softly lit the doctor's study lamp and rifled the drawer of the old secretaire and the cash-box it holds. He has seized The Diamond from the Sky and clasped it in agitated ecstasy upon his bosom. At last the wonderful Stanleigh heirloom was his. Since he was a little todiller his mother had been bemoaning the loss of the jewel, and now it is his —his. Arthur Stanleigh is welcome to the title. " This will repay old scores!" hisses the young man as the memory of tales of the old feud comes into his mind. Then upon his guilty ears falls the strain of a guitar. He hides the diamond and the chain and locket that holds it under his shirt. Meanwhile, sleeping the light slumber of the aged, the old doctor had been aroused by the open- ing of the window. Candle in hand, he descended to his study, and entered just as the last gleam from the diamond was shrouded by the shirt of the thief in the night. There was a quick, sharp struggle. Frenzied with fear, and resolved to do murder rather than lose the long-coveted heirloom, Blair Stanleigh, maddened with rage, strangled the doctor till the weak old man fell across the writing- table, his heart stopped in death. Then the mur- derer turned to sneak from the room. But he paused. Overhead he heard movemente. Esther had been aroused, he thought, by the noise of the struggle. He must not be seen by the girl, and he moved to the open window, where the strains from the guitar, strains of a love song, tinkled in the night.

THE EI.AMINER, SATURDAY, JUNE 10, 1916.

FARM AND GARDEN.

THE HAY HARVEST.

A confirmation of the optimistic reports re- garding the prospect of the season's hay crop was obtained in a run through some of the home counties lately. There seems good reason (says "Farm and Home") for the expectation that so far as the yield per acre is concerned the crop will be most satisfactory. Many fields present a very luxurious bottom growth, and if, as is believed, at least as large an area as usual of the grass land has been kept for haying, the crop should be a very large one. In the Thames valley the fields present a thick close growth, and an abundance of feed was evident on the grazing lands. _ _

The recent warm weather and welcome showers have done much to ensure a good growing season. Not only for farm crops, how- ever, but also for weeds, and here it is that much labour will be entailed this month that can ill be spared if root crops are to be kept clean. The horse-hoe will need to be used as often and as long as possible, but if not, hand- hoeing must be resorted to, for there is a great amount of truth in the adage that "one year's -seeding means seven years' weeding."

===== 0002601_19160610_art0134.txt | included=true | included =====
MARKETING POTATOES.

The movement set on foot among the potato growers of Lancashire and Cheshire to secure a common undertaking among farmers,.to.send the, early potato crop to 4he market unsorted, is not supported by the merchants who, at the same time, are not disposed to regard the proposal as serious. In the "Preston Guar- dian" M. A. C. Bradshaw, of Messrs. B. Ball and Sons, Preston, characterises the proposal as ridiculous, and says he cannot imagine that it emanated from farmers with extensive crop- ping areas. The loss to the grower in the market value of unsorted potatoes would, he said, be out of all proportion to the little extra labour involved in picking. The riddling out of the seeds and chats from the ware' potatoes has always been regarded as a most important process, and it is a very common thing for merchants to insist on potatoes being put over a 2in. riddle. And where- the riddle is used there is not a good deal of work involved. There are sorting machines that will greatly economise labour.

===== 0002601_19160610_art0135.txt | included=true | included =====
WEEDS AND THEIR SUPPRESSION.

The Board of Agriculture desire to call the special attention of farmers to the great need for combating weeds, which are usually respon sible for heavy loss in the yield of crops. At the present time it is most important that the maximum yield of foodstuffs should be obtained from the land, and this can only be done by keeping the land free from weeds, and in a high state of cultivation, and by sowing pure seed of high germinating power. The present leaflet is devoted to a general consideration of weeds and their eradication.

• In this connection it may be remarked that in a memorandum issued last year by the Farmers' Club it is observed that, "The biggest waste in agriculture is caused by weeds. As a rule, weeds are permitted by bad farmers only, and a determined attempt, notwithitanding all difficulties, should be made to ;!,•et rid of the weeds so that the yield per acre of the crops we grow may attain the highest standard." Damage Done By Weeds.

The most serious objections to weeds may he stated as follows:

They absorb from the soil moisture and plant food which would otherwise go to nourish and increase the crop which is being cultivated. They " crowd " the crop, restricting the amount of light, heat and air necessary for healthy growth and for the proper assimilation of plant food. The effect is to hamper the growth of the plants during early life, especially in the case of crops of slow growth, while they prevent or retard ripening and drying, par- ticularly in the case of corn crops. The straw of cereals may be weakened and rendered liable to "lodge," thus making the work of cutting at harvest both difficult and expensive. Weeds, especially such climbing kinds as bind- weed and cleavers, hamper harvesting of corn crops, both as regards cutting and drying. Weeds interfere with, and render more ex- pensive, proper and thorough cultivation, and the " singling " of root crops. Weeds may harbour, 'or...favour the develop- ment of, insect and fUngns pests. The value of samples of cereals is reduced by the presence of cockle, garlic; cleavers, and wild vetch; the value of most farm seeds is lowered by the presence of the seeds of weeds; and the market value of hay and other farm produce is similarly reduced by certain weeds or their seeds.

Some weeds—e.g., garlic—taint the milk of cows which eat them, while others—e.g., meadow saffron and water hemlock—are poison- ous to stock generally.

Other weeds (dodder, broomrape, yellow rattle) are parasitic or semi-parasitic, and directly feed upon the crops they infest. The underground stems and roots of weeds may cause the stoppage of drains.

Most farmers recognise that it is impossible to obtain the best returns from the land when weeds are allowed to grow unchecked. Experi- ment has shown that on a properly weeded area of arable land the crop may be double that on an unweeded area; e.g., in one case mangolds, grown under otherwise exactly similar con- ditions on the same field, yielded 371 tons per acre where two hoeings were given, and only 161 tons where there was no weeding after singling.

===== 0002601_19160610_art0136.txt | included=true | included =====
IN THE GARDEN.

CLAY DRESSING FOR LIGHT LAND.

In the garden the addition of clay to light, sandy soils does not, I think, receive the atten- tion it deserves. The value of a dressing of clayoto such soils, in many of which good crops cannot be grown, is very apparent. Some people run away with the idea that all that such poor soils require is plenty Of manure. This is a mistake, as sandy soils are very hungry, and manure does but little good. In- stead of manuring try a dressing of clay and note the difference when the crops come to maturity. The clay is beet wheeled on to the ground during the winter, f5O that tire- frost may have time to do its work. Not only in the kitchen garden, but also for beds and borders where the soil is dry and hot, a dresis- ing of clay will work wonders.

===== 0002601_19160610_art0137.txt | included=true | included =====
DWARF NASTURTIUMS.

The town dweller who is fond of flowers is at a great disadvantage, as his window boxes and other receptacles are not only exposed to the full force of the sun and the heat radiating from the walls on all sides, but are affected by the fact,that the surrounding bricks dry up every particle of moisture. Under these con- ditions it is useless to plant many things that, given normal conditions, would do well, but which would be a failure in such surroundings. For such a purpose few things are so•useful as the Nasturtium, which, however hot the posi- tion may be, will hold its own and flower freely. For the A'asturtium the soil should not be too rich, as the plants flower all the better if the soil is on the poor side, while they do not suffer if the watering should be neglected. Sow the seeds where the plants are to flower, and should the seedlings be too thick it is an easy matter to thin them down to the necessary number.

===== 0002601_19160610_art0138.txt | included=true | included =====
WASTE OF SEEDS.

A mistake all too 'often made is sowing seeds too thickly. This is not only a waste of seeds, for crowded rows are most unprofitable. 'Of course, sufficient seeds should be sown to allow a margin of losses due to the attacks of birds or slugs, but this is no reason why the seed- lings should come up so thickly that they spoil each other, and, in spite of an early thinning, jeopardise the crop. A word of advice may here be given. It is true, economy to purchase seeds from a reliable source, as the very cheap packets of seed are very often not true to name, are old, or badly ripened, and, however care- fully sown, disappoint,—" Wick," in "Farm and Home."

ANGLING.

I,VARRINGTON WORKING MEN'S ASSOCIATION.

N.A. CENTRE.

The result of the National Federation meeting at Leeds, on the Northern Anglers' proposition, re renting or loaning waters for stock fish, wati a very close thing. The voting was: First show of hands, eight for and eight against. The chairman decided upon another vote being taken, not caring to give a casting vote. Result: Nine for, eleven against. After hearing Mr. Lees' report we shall be sur- prised if some similar scheme does not carry at the meat meeting, he (Mr. Lees) pointing out that centres in the N.A. alone were prepared to spend a matter of 200 if they could buy healthy and size- able fish. •

As there was £44 collected over the amount re- quired for the two motor ambulances, the surplus will go in tackle for soldiers serving abroad. The question of the price of travelling cards to centres was not thrashed out. and_wilL cotha up at another meeting. _

The opening match from.headquarters on June 25th, at Aintree, seems to find favour amongSt mem- bers, and we hear there will be a useful lot of prizes. The Bay Horse is following the centre's lead and bringing their match forward. It is sug- gested to hold it on the second Saturday after the headquarter's match, in the Norton Canal, but this is not confirmed news as yet.

NOTICE. Club night at headquarters on Monday next for contributions, Scc. General meeting at Bay Horse Monday next at 7.30. Please attend, as there is an important agenda. A.S.

IN THE COURTS.

DEFIED THE PRIEST.

Scotch 'Woman's Habits Lead To

Prosecution.

Prosecuting Mrs. Mary Hoey, of 20, Dale- street, for neglecting her six children, Mr. Arthur Browne, on behalf of the N.S.P.C.C., at the Borough Police Court on Tuesday, said that the defendant was the wife of a furnace- man who worked at Whitecross Works, and was in the receipt of excellent wages. Before com- ing to Warrington about fifteen months ago, they lived at Moss End, Lanarkshire, and one of the clergy there had written that Mr. Hoey bore a very high character. Drink, however, had been the downfall of Mrs. Hoey, and the house was in a deplorable condition—filthy, and devoid of furniture, owing to Mrs. Hoey having pawned everything she could lay her hands on. There was no need for her to pawn anything, be- cause her husband gave her 325. and paid the rent each week. That she should be a victim of the drink was a source of great sorrow to her husband, who had remarked to one of the clergy that he "would give a fortune if he could change her habits.' In conclusion, Mr. Browne said that the defendant defied everybody, in- cluding the priest. Inspector Wainwright, of the N.S.P.C.C. and Detective-Sergeant Reddington, gave evidence confirming Mr. Browne's statement, and the magistrates adjourned the case for four weeks in the hope that the defendant would improve.

===== 0002601_19160610_art0150.txt | included=true | included =====
ROPE MANUFACTURER

Fined For Assaulting Member Of Board Of Guardians.

A business friend for many years. George Allen, a member of the Board of Guardians, and of the firm of Messrs. George Allen and Sons, of Ellesmere-street, prosecuted at the Borough Police Court on Tuesday, Joseph Craik, a rope manufacturer; of Grey-street, for common asasult.

Mr. T. S. Steel, who appeared for the com- plainant, said the defendant suffered from illusions owing to his habit of drinking, and had made certain allegations against his wife, saying that she was too familiar with Mr. Allen and, as a consequence, Mrs. Craik rang up Mr. Allen and asked him to come next day and refute the aspersions. As there was not the slightest foundation for the suggestion either re- garding Mr. Allen or anybody else. Mr. Allen went to the house and, on his arrival, Mr. Craik grasped him by the neck with both hands and endeavoured to strangle him, holding him for several minutes. Mr. Allen then rushed out of the house and informed his brother. Mr. Allen's firm, concluded Mr. Steel, had done work for the defendant for a great number of years, and it was most unfair and cowardly on the part of the defendant to act in the way he did when the complainant went to meet the charges made.

Evidence in support was given by Mr. Allen and Mrs. Craik, and then Mr. Arthur Browne, for the defence, said the defendant did not agree that he asked Mrs. Craik to send for Mr. Allen. When the latter came and saw that Mrs. Craik was not in the room he turned to bolt for the door and the defendant got hold of him to detain him. That was all. Mr. Browne added that he made no charge against Mrs. Craik or Mr. Allen. Fine: 205., and an advocate's fee.

===== 0002601_19160610_art0151.txt | included=true | included =====
SOLDIERS' WIVES' RENT ARREARS.

Applications To Distrait' Refused By

Magistrates.

The wives of two of our local soldiers appeared to answer summonses -against their husbands at the Police Court on Wednesday, before Mr. J. W. -Hayward:, (in the chair). -Arrears of rent was the cause of the trouble, and an application by the agent of the properties was made to ills- train.

One of them, with five young children, whose husband is .in the National Reserve, it was stated was in receipt of an Army allowance of 275. 6d. per week, and had failed to pay her rent of 4s. weekly. During the last two months she had only paid 55., and the arrears totalled £2 Is. id.

Defendant informed the collector that she had been advised by the authorities at Parr Hall not to pay the rent if she could not afford it. On inquiry this statement was found to be in- correct, as the officials were of the opinion that she could pay the rent regularly. The Chairman: Are you prepared to make any offer?

Defendant: What can you do out of 275. Cd. with five little children? After paying my way I have only 2s. left. The collector said it was a case of considerable importance, as the Government allowance was paid in full, yet they were unable to obtain the rent. They would have to pay ground rent, rates and other taxes.

The Magistrates' Clerk (Mr. G. White): Can YOU not put her in as tenant? As the husband has been away twelve months they could not get at him.

The application was refused by the magis- trates, but the defendant was advised by the magistrates to pay the rent regularly. In the second case the circumstances were somewhat similar, but the amount of arrears was £4. The woman admitted to the collector that it cost her 3s. weekly to send cigarettes to her husband. In the opinion of the collector there appeared to be unnecessary extravagance. Before the husband joined the Army it was stated that they .were not very exemplary tenants.

The application to distrain was refused.

FINED FOR CATCHING WILD BIRDS.

Two linnets and, a red-pole chirped merrily in the County Sessions Police Court on Wed- nesday, whilst their owner, John Webb, of 96, Liverpool-road, appeared to answer a charge of catching wild birde• during' the prohibited season- The birds in Court Were used as a " decoy " by the defendant when bird catching on the river bank near Sankey. Inspector Dee, of the N.S.P.C.A., stated that on May 10th he saw the defendant crouching behind some bushes which had been smothered with bird lime twigs. Three birds in cages were placed near the bushes in order to lure other members of the feathered tribe. Defend- ant said he had caught three redpoles, but had liberated them. A fine of 15s. was imposed.

WARNING TO STONE-THROWERS.

The damage to telephone insulators by boys throwing stones on the Cheshire Lines Railway was estimated at over £3O during the hearing of a case at the Juvenile Court on Wednesday, when seven boys were fined for wilful damage. A Sunday or two ago a kite belonging to one of the boys was caught in the telephone wires on the railway. In the attempt to release it the boys threw stones and a number of insu- lators were damaged in consequence. P.C. Joynson, of the Chehire Lines Police, saw .the affair and caught one of the boys, who gave the names of the others. It was stated that during the past nine weeks no less than 1,000 insulators had been broken, which had to be replaced by the railway com- pany at a cost of ninepence each. Apart from the damage: the boys -ran great risk by tres- passing. on- the railway property. Six of the boys were fined ss„ and the other, .who was working, had. to pay 78. Gd.

ENCOURAGING YOUTHFUL GAMBLERS. Six youths,- named Mitchell, Farrington, Far- rington, Sinnett, 0. Day and Fleming, all resid- ing in the neighbourhood of Ileatan-square, were summoned at the Police Court on Wednesday fof playing " banker " with cards for money.— Detective Turner, who effected their capture on Saturday afternoon, May 27th, said the defend- ants were playing on the doorstep at the rear of the houses in the square. The parents of the boys appeared to take no notice of them, and allowed gambling to go on.-0. Day and Sinnctt were each fined 7s. 6d. and the others ss. each.

THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, JUNE 10, 1916.

Situations Vacant.

===== 0002601_19160610_art0166.txt | included=true | included =====
THE TRIBUNALS.

ONE LOCAL SITTING THIS HEARD AT LIVERPOOL. WEEK.

Only one sitting of the Borough Tribunal was held this week—on Tuesday, when the time was chiefly occupied in dealing with claims on busi- ness grounds. The members of the Tribunal present were the Deputy Mayor, who presided over the main portion of the proceedings, Messrs. F. A. Frost, J. J. Whitley and W. Brad- bury. Did Not Want to be a Laughing Stock.

A single young man in business on his own account, who stated that there was no one de- pendent upon him at home, appealed on busi- ness grounds. Since the last hearing he had failed to dispose of his business. Apart from his appeal he was not fit for military service, as'he had no hair on his head. He would look nice in uniform and would be a laughing stock. —The Clerk (Mr. A. T. Hallaway) : You are not going butting. (Laughter.)—Applicant: It does not matter about butting. I have to be looked at, haven't I? It is no laughing matter. He further stated he would not care if he could sell the business, but he could not.—The Chairman (Dr. Joseph): That is the only hardship in the case.—Applicant: What am I to do when I come back again? It has taken me six years to build up the business, and it would be ruined.—Two months' exemption was granted, to be regarded as final. He was told to appeal within three days if he was dissatisfied with the decision. Single Men's Domestic Responsibilities.

The domestic responsibilities of a single young man, who claimed that his father had not worked for 20 years, and that he was the sole support, next -occupied the attention of the Tribunal. He was the proprietor of an Aunt Sally stall.—The appeal was refused, the appli- cant being advised that the new regulations would enable him to remove any hardship in his case.

A similar case was heard, in which a single young man claimed to be the support of his aged parents, his father being an invalid. If he had to go it was not financial hardship alone, but the fact that his parents were not able to look after themselves very well.—The Chair- man: You could not look after them whilst at work.—Applicant: I know they would grieve if I had to go.—The claim was disallowed, appli- cant being informed that for any hardship he would be able to appeal under the new regula- tions. _

===== 0002601_19160610_art0167.txt | included=true | included =====
Milk Dealers' Difficulties.

Mr. Jacob Sankey, jun., appealed in respect of their only male employe engaged in the milk business, a married man- with three children. He understood it was a reserved occupation.— The Clerk: That is wrong; it was altered two months ago.—Continuing, Mr. Sankey said they could not do without one man in the business. Lifting tankards of milk, usually weighing about one cwt., was not a position for a woman. They had three employes before the war, but two, aged 19 and 32 respectively, left to go into munition works. They were situated rather differently from other milk dealers. They did not do the heavy work themselves or look after the • three horses.—Captain Mathams: Why?— Mr. Sankey: I have another business to look after and spend three hours each day with the milk business. There is no other male employe. If we could do without him we would not appeal for him: About eighteen months ago we adver- tised for a man and tried two or three, but they were not satisfactory. Married men over mili- tary age will not leave their present employ- ment unless they are guaranteed a permanent job. If men are released for the Army we like to leave their job open until they return.—Two months' exemption was granted, with the advice that a substitute should be found.

Long Working Days in a Long Week.

A wholesale newsagent and tobacconist, mar- ried, with one child, appealed on btsiness and domestic grounds. His wife had been an in- valid for four years. She was, a cripple and could not walk without the aid of sticks.—Con- ditional exemption granted, and applicant was asked to join the Volunteer Training Corps.— Applicant: I work sixteen hours per day and seven days to the week. If I joined I would have to get someone in the shop' whilst I am away.—The Chairman: You would put more than sixteen hours per day in the Army.— Applicant': I will do my best. , Greengrocer's Appeal. The business appeal of a wholesale gretn- grocer was adjourned for a fortnight.—The ap- plicant stated that his assistant, for whom he appealed, was called up last week; but had been rejected as unfit. He could not manage the business in his absence, as he was no scholar. Tribunal's Thanks for Advice.

A single man, with a widowed mother and a delicate sister, who appealed on domestic grounds, informed the Tribunal that he thought as he had been passed for home service only, he would be doing better service for his country by staying at home and looking after his mother. There are " tons " of men in the barracks who would be only too pleased to go abroad.—The Chairman: We are not here to discuss that.— Applicant: I think you ought to do.—The Chairman: We are much obliged for that sug- gestion.—One month's exemption was granted so that a doctor could appear to give evidence as to the condition of the applicant's sister.

Conditional Exemption.

Conditional exemption was granted a whole- sale fruit and potato salesman, who has been in business on his own account for eighteen years.—Mr. T. S. Steel appeared for the appel- lant, who stated that formerly he had five drivers, and all had joined the Colours. He was now left on his own to carry on the busi- ness. Another brother joined the Army last week, and applicant had undertaken to assist his wife in the carrying on of his business dur- ing his absence. Tribunal Want as Many Men as Possible. Mr. T. S. Steel, on behalf of Messrs. John Toft and Sons, bakers, Bridge-street, applied for the exemption of two married employes, engaged as horse-keeper and driver respectively. It was pointed out that since the outbreak of war three drivers had joined the forces, and they had re- placed them by engaging boys of sixteen years of age. In the case of the horse-keeper, it was his duty to clean and harness the horses, because the boys were unable to do it. The second em- ploye, a driver, had been with the firm for nine- teen years. The Chairman: If the other vans can be driven by boys, why cannot you pursue the policy to its conclusion? Mr. Steel explained that the man also carted flour and other goods from the station. As the firm did their own carting before the war, the railway company declined to undertake it now they were pressed for men. The Chairman: Could you not spare one of these men? You know the position the Tri- bunal are in. We want to get as many men for the Army as possible and justly as we can. Mr. Steel said his client had made great sacri- fices, and he was .not going to press the Tri- bunal unduly. With regard to the driver, they were prepared to make an endeavour to replace him.

Conditional exemption was granted to the horse-keeper and one month's exemption for the driver.

Messrs. Carter and Son's Claims.

Messrs. Carter and Son, bakers, confectioners, corn and flour dealers, Bridge-street, obtained conditional exemption for three bakers, the shop manager, and the hoistman in the ware- house. Two months' exemption was granted to two confectioners, and three months' to a motor van driver. An appeal in respect of a baker was refused, as he is a single man be- low the minimum age for exemption.

The Chairman Oh:. F. A. Frost), in announc- ing the decision, said they had given all pos- sible weight to the record of the firm in the matter of recruiting. It was stated that the firm had 26 men of military age prior to the outbreak of war, and now they had only 15. The only director of military age had received a commission in the Army. The Tribunal suggested that it might be possible to train women as confectioners.

Warrington Market Prices. At Wedro.sday's Warrington Market We price of wheat ruled at 7s. 3d. to 7s. 4d. per 701 b., and 4s. 4d. to 4s. 6d. was realised for oats. Flour realised 425. 6d. to 475. 6d. Per 2801 b., 2s. Id. to 2s. sd. being the price of thirds. The price of bran and other offals was Is. 53d. to Is. Bd.

===== 0002601_19160610_art0168.txt | included=true | included =====
Appeals By Business Men And Military Authorities.

R.S.P.C.A. INSPECTOR TO GO.

Several Warrington appeals were heard at Liverpool on Thursday afternoon, the Lord. Mayor presiding. Some were made by em- ployers and men themselves; while not a few were put forward by the military representa- tive. _ _

It was the latter who appealed against the decision of the Warrington Local Tribunal in granting conditional exemption to the War- rington and district inspector under the R.S.P.C.A. Mr. Steel appeared for the society.

The appeal was on ground that the inspector was engaged in purely normal pre- ventive work of patrolling streets for cases of cruelty and his services in the Army would be much more valuable at the present time. The local Tribunal were of opinion that the work of the society was one which should not be allowed to drop. _ .

Mr. Steel pointed out that 50 per cent. of the inspectors of the society had joined the Army, and those who were left were doing double work.

The Military Representative: In the present stress it could be done by the police and special constables. Mr. Steel: These inspectors have had a veterinary training. My client has a very ex- tensive district round Warrington. Private individuals will make reports to him more readily than they will to the police. The Lord Mayor: We allow this appeal. We think he would be more useful in the Army. Tradespeople Support Motor Mechanic.

William J. Lake appealed on behalf of a Motor mechanic. Mr. T. S. Steel explained that Mr. Lake carried on a motor taxi busi- ness, being also a repairer of motor cars and vehicles. The mechanic was the only one he had, his other two having joined the Army. This one was altogether indispensable. He did 'a great deal of repairing, especially of commercial vehicles, and for the past three months had been engaged in looking after Cor- poration motor 'buses. The Lord Mayor intimated that the War- rington Tribunal explained that they had al- ready given conditional exemption to Mr. Lake. Mr. Lake pointed out that owing to the shortage of men women were being more and more employed on commercial vehicles, and, consequently, the drivers were not so handy at repairs and brought them to his (Mr. Lake's) premises to be put right. Mr. Steel produced a letter signed by several tradespeople in the town, declaring that they would be much inconvenienced if they could not get their repairs done by Mr. Lake. Conditional exemption was granted. A Contracting Carter.

Mr. Arthur Browne represented Messrs. Joseph Caldwell and Sons, carters, of Win- wick-street, in appealing for a son, aged 23 years of age. The firm were under contract to cart for a large tannery company, and the secretary had sent a letter stating that the man was indispensable to their output. In answer to the Lord Mayor, it was stated that there were four sons, one of whom had enlisted, another was engaged on munitions, and another had been permanently injured in an accident.—The appeal was dismissed.

===== 0002601_19160610_art0169.txt | included=true | included =====
A Month's Extension.

Supporting the appeal of Mr. William Mason, 33, .Orford-lane (property repairer, rent• col- lector and coffin maker) for his only employe, Mr. W. B. Forshaw said that Mr. Mason had broken a leg, and, complications having set in, it would be three -months before ,he could get about. Before the war -began Mr. Mason em- ployed two men and a boy, and the young man he had left was necessary to keep the business together, especially while Mr. Mason was ill. It was ,admitted that most of the work con- sisted of collecting rents, and this the local Tribunal considered could be dote: by .Older men or women.

The military representative mentioned that Mr. Mason had two sons making_ munitions.

Mr. Forshaw : Both are married.

The local Tribunal- had granted exemption until the 30th June, which. the Appeal Tribunal extended to the 31st July. Horse Slaughterer's Temporary Exemption.

An exemption of two nionths was given a carter in the employ of the Warrington Pro- ducts Company. A representative explained that it was the man's duty to shoot condemned cattle and bring them to the premises. He could not be replaced. The company was so short-handed—it only had seven men left—that part of their plant stood idle. In reply to the military representative the company's manager was understood to say that on an average seventeen horses were slaught- ered every week in Warrington and district. If this man goes, are you going to give up horse slaughtering?—l shall have to do.

Exempted for tN.i:o months. "Try to get some- body," advised the Lord Mayor.

Decision Reversed.

The local Tribunal having allowed an employe of Messrs. J. Pendlebury and Co. three months' respite, the military representative appealed. It was stated that the man was 29 years of age, had worked with the firm for 15 years, was a. traveller and bookkeeper in the coal depart- ment, and assisted the manager of the house furnishing department. ' The military repre- sentative pointed out that out of nine men in the business, two only were of serviceable age: Mr. Fred Pendlebury said that he had a large coal business, having to deal with from three to four hundred waggons per month. There was only one man left to look after the furnish- ing shop. • Military representative's appeal allowed. Army Relief Man not Arrived.

Benjamin Cross, butcher, of 2, Corporation- street, appealed, through Mr. T. S. Steel, against the refusal of exemption to his son, who is a journeyman butcher and slaughterman. Prior to the ivar the appellant had two men, two youths, and a casual workman, but after the outbreak of war the two men left, and subse- quently the two youths went away. The appel- lant now only had the attested man, a casual workman and a boy left. Appellant had been under medical treatment since July. Two days of each week he had to go out of town buying live meat for slaughter.

The local Tribunal, in refusing the applica- tion, stated that the son was 20 years of age, and were of opinion that in a case like that the age should be a deciding factor. Mr. Steel added that the son assisted in the killing. There was no public abattoir in War- rington. • The Military Representative (to appellant): What happened to the ex-soldier butcher sent you from the recruiting office? ' Mr. Cross: He never arrived! Appeal dismissed. Decision Confirmed Regarding Plumber. •

The appeal of a plumber and decorator (mar- ried with one child) was dismissed. Before the war he had two men and two boys, but he could not get any now, and wanted to keep the busi- ness going on his own, though he was working under difficulties. The local Tribunal had re- fused his application for exemption on the ground that the business was very small, and there would be little difficulty in working the business up again. The Appeal Tribunal con- firmed their decision.

Memorial Service.' A most impressive memorial service for three members of St. Paul's Church Lads' Brig/tie who have met their deaths while serving their country, namely, corporal J. Renshaw, Bugler W. Gibson, and Private George Hart, was held at St. Paul's Church on Sunday morning. Despite the inclement weather there was an at- tendance e over 100 lads, comprising the St. f Paul's and St. Elphin's Companies. The officers in charge were Major J. Brookes, Captain F. Bunch, and Lieutenant N. Morris. The drums were massed before the altar, and the Union Jack placed over them. The Rev. E. Downham conducted the service, and also preached on the words ." Be thou Aztithful unto death and I will give thee a crown of life." Special hymns were sung, and suitable prayers offered. After the blessing the "Last Post" was sounded and the National Anthem sung.

Private Forsyth, who is 18 years of age, was formerly employed at Guest's Tannery, Win- wick-street. He was educated, at Silver-street School, and was a member of St. Peter's Church Young Men's Bible Class.

BROTHERS DIE FOR THEIR COUNTRY.

News has reached Mr. and Mrs. F. Broad- hurst, 31, Wellington-street, that two of their .sons—Private John Broadhurst, of the Seaforth Highlanders, and Private James Broadhurst, of the Scottish Borderers—have met their death while serving their country. The first- named died in hospital on 29th May from dysentery, whilst James is officially reported to have met his death on the 25th September. He had been reported missing from that date, until his parents received an official message a week or two ago. Private John Broadhurst, who was "23 years of age, enlisted in September, 1914. He had been previously employed by the Vernon- street Tanning Company. He was educated at the National Day School and was an enthu- siastic follower of football.

Private James Broadhurst, who was 18 years of age, was formerly employed by the Rich- mond Gas Stove and Meter Company at their Grappenhall Works. He also was educated at the Church-street Schools.

Another brother, Private H. Broadhurst, formerly belonged to the local Territorials. He was wounded and gassed in the severe fighting in June, and was afterwards dis- charged as medically. unfit.

===== 0002601_19160610_art0171.txt | included=true | included =====
WOUNDED.

CORPOR 1L SAM REYNOLDS.

Wounded In The Left Hand By Shrapnel Bullets.

Corporal Samuel Reynolds, King's Royal Rifles, son of Mr. G. N. and Mrs. Reynolds, 117, Marsh House-lane, has been wounded for the second time, and is now in the 3rd Canadian General Hospital in France. Corporal Cecil' Fleming, of the K.R.R., writing to Mrs. Reynolds last Saturday, said that her son had been slightly wounded in the left hand that morning. The bullet made a nasty gash, but he had had every attention, and there was no need to worry. , "All the boys in his company will miss him very much," says the letter. Rifleman Frank V. Wood, also of the same company, writes to say he left Sam quite fit and well in the trenches on Friday, but on Saturday he learnt that he had had three fingers of his left hand injured by shrapnel. "It seems very 'strange," he adds, "being the first time either of us has been in the trenches without the other. I was sent out to go through a seven days' bombing course. . . . I have lost an excel- lent chum, and do not like the idea of rejoining my company and not finding cheerful old Sam waiting for me." The hospital chaplain, writing on Monday,

TORPEDOMAN HACK WELL. A:Veil And Safe On Tiger Alter The Battle. Mr. and Mrs- J. Hackwell, 137, Oldham- street, Latchford, on Sunday received a postcard from their son, Seaman Torpedoman James Henry Hackwell, of H.M.S. Tiger, who stated that he was quite well and safe after the recent action. Torpedoman Ilackwell, who is 21 years of age, has been in the Navy almost four years, and in addition to the Tiger, has served on H.M.S. Irresistible, Skirmisher and Defiant. Previous to joining the Navy, he was employed as a moulder by Messrs. Fletcher, Russell and co.

jliterature or undesirable films was not stated, ' at the Juvenile Court on Wednesday, when they were charged with stealing a silver casket, razor blade and other goods, value 55., the property of Agnes Lavin, 12, Cross-street. The Chief Constable said that an entrance was effected through the back kitchen window, which was securely fastened when, Mrs. Lavin left the house. On her return the kitchen was found in a state of disorder. Drawers had been ransacked and the contents strewn about the floor. A number of articles were missing. The parents of one of the boys resided next door to the prosecutrix, and a portion of the stam property was returned by the boy's father, in- cluding the small silver casket and the razor blade. The boys subsequently admitted to Detective Skelton having stolen the property. TRAM(I3) SHELTER. A tramp who was found by P.C. Hawkins sleeping in the tram shelter at the Cemetery terminus early on Wednesday morning, was sent to gaol for fourteen days by the magistrates at Wednesday's Police Court. ALLEGED FALSE PRETENCES. A remand of a week was granted yesterday, at the Borough Police Court, in the case of Jane Davies, alias Marshall, a van dweller, who appeared in the dock on a charge of ob- taining 17s. 6d. and a quantity of groceries by fAlse pretences from Mary Jane Clay, 164, Win- wick-road.—The Chief Constable stated that prisoner went to the shop of the prosecutrix on Tuesday and represented that she had been recommended to go there for home-made bread. She further said that she would be able to recommend fourteen families to deal with her for provisions and groceries. Prisoner then asked the prosecutrix to purchase a rug and asked 30s. for it. Eventually she pur- chased the rug for 17s. 6d. and the prisoner had groceries amounting to 10s., on the under- standing that she would get the custom which the prisoner said she would bring. Prisoner went away and was arrested by Detective- Sergeant Sumner at Frodsham. Subsequently two men went to the prosecutrix's shop and paid 275. 6d., in the belief they were going to help the prisoner. The Chief Constable added that Mr. Steel had been instructed on behalf of the prisoner, and he applied for a week's remand, which was granted.

ALL TOGETHER. Farmers Not Going To Separate Potatoes. A meeting of farmers from Warrington and surrounding districts, including Frodsham, Northwich, Chester, Knutsford, Ormskirk, St. Helens and Wigan, was held at the Blue Bell Inn, Horsemarket-street, on Wednesday, to consider the question of the lifting of potato crops. The Secretary (Mr. J. W. Dyke) reported that the movement was entered upon by a few farmers who foresaw the advantage that would be (rained if a scheme could be adopted where- by 'the potatoes could be got without being sorted out as in previous years. At a recent meeting the farmers were urged to put on the market the whole of the potato crops without separating the smaller ones. The question of later raising by Lancashire and Cheshire was brought forward by Mr. Norcott, and it was proposed that all potatoes going to the market be thrown together, and that the same be carried into effect until such time as potatoes are put in the pit. The Co-operative Society were also in favour, as also were almost all the largest potato growers. If they would all cling together, there was no reason why the matter should not be a success. Several farmers who had attended recent meetings in different districts—Wigan, Knuts- ford, St.! Helens, Northwich, Preston Brook, and Stretton—said most of the farmers were in agreement with the scheme. A Farmer: What about seed? September will be too late for it. The Chairman (Mr. Marsh): The suggestion is that the seed be thrown out and the others left. After some discussion the following resolution was passed: "That this meeting of farmers, ! comprising Warrington, Northwich, FrOdsharn, Knutsford, Ormskirk, Wigan, St. Helens and !Liverpool and all other neighbouring districts, support the new movement re throwing the whole of the potato crops together until the end of September, and urges upon all the farm- ing community the vital necessity, owing to the great dearth of labour, &c., of strong co- operation in securing for the movement their unanimous support."

ON HONOUR'S SCROLL. DIED. PRIVATE T. J. WILLIAMS. Official News Received That He Has Died In Hospital. Official news has now reached Mr. and Mrs. T. Williams, 43, Longford-street, that their son, Private Thomas Jolley Williams, of the South Lancashire Regiment, has died of wounds. As we reported last week, Mr. and Mrs. Williams were first informed that their son had died of wounds in hospital, but the following day Private Williams him§elf wrote that he had been wounded, but was progressing favourably. On Friday, however, a telegram was received to the effect that he was dangerously ill, whilst later another one came from the Record Office at Shrewsbury officially announcing his death in hospital on June 2nd. Private Williams, who was 21 years of age, enlisted on the Ist September, 1914, having been previously employed as a moulder by Messrs. Fletcher, Russell and Co., Ltd., where he had worked since leaving school. He was drafted to the Dardanelles in June, and in November was sent home with frost-bitten feet. He was an in- mate of the Netley Hospital for a month, and, after recovering, was sent to the theatre of war, in which he has met his death. He was educatad at St. Ann's School'. His father, who is a wire- drawer by trade, but who has had to relinquish that occupation through ill-health, enlisted the same week as his son, but was discharged after five weeks' service. He is at present an emplove of the Alliance Box Company, Ltd., Orford-lane. CORPORAL F. ISHERWOOD. Dies In Cairo Hospital From • Appendicitis. Official news was received on Wednesday by Mr. and Mrs: H. Isherwood, Heathfield, Fair- field-road, Latchford Without, of the death of their eldest son, Corporal Frank Forster Isherwood, M.T., A.S.C., in hospital at Cairo from appendicitis. Corporal Isherwood, who was in the Water Engineer's Office, enlisted in the second month of the war, was promoted to full Corporal last December, and in January went to Egypt. For some weeks past he had been Acting-Sergeant of his section. He was 22 years of age. No details are yet to hand as to his illness. Mr. and Mrs. Isherwood's second son, S. H. Isherwood, is a Sapper in the Royal Anglesey Royal Engineers, and for the past nine months has been on active service in France.

''..."-•••••••.::::: • i::::.•‘....' • '•14`.....4•*1 . . .:„. . ~..,....,,,.- , , -. ' -e••• , PRIVATEwouGn. Wounded. WRIGHT. -

Private Wright was struck in the leg and shoulder after finishing writing a letter to his wife. He is now in hospital. . Private Wright, who is a native of Liverpool, has been in the Army twelve months, and at the Front six months. His father is attached to the National Reserve, while his brother is a Cor- poral in the A.S.C. Three brothers-in-law are also serving, one of them being a local Terri- torial. LOCAL CASUALTIES. In the lists of casualties published during the. week we have noticed with regret the following: local names Killed. King's (Liverpool) li4•l.7imont.—J. W. liumph- reys, 11888. Wounded. Royal Irish Rifles.—W. Welch, 5165. Loyal North Lancs. Begiment.—H.„ Brooks, , 13562. Particulars Required. , As it is difficult for us to trace .some of the' casualties in the lists we should appreciate the assistance of friends and relatives. We insert • the fullest information and photographs of local men on being supplied.
